Irreducibility of the Koopman representations for the group GL 0 ​ ( 2 ​ ∞ , ℝ ) {\rm GL}_{0}(2\infty,{\mathbb{R}}) acting on three infinite rows Journal: Journal of Functional Analysis To all fearless Ukrainians defending not only their country, but the whole civilization against putin’s rashism
A.V. Kosyak, P. Moree

Abstract
In [25] the first author started with the development of harmonic analysis on infinite-dimensional groups. In this article, following these ideas, we construct an analogue of quasi-regular representations, when the group G G acts on a G − G- space X X equipped with a quasi-invariant measure. For the group G G we take the inductive limit of the general linear groups GL 0 ​ ( 2 ​ ∞ , ℝ ) {\rm GL}_{0}(2\infty,{\mathbb{R}}) = lim → n ⁡ GL ⁡ ( 2 ​ n − 1 , ℝ ) =\varinjlim_{n}{\rm GL}(2n\!-\!1,{\mathbb{R}}) , acting on the space X m X_{m} of m m rows, infinite in both directions, with Gaussian measure. This measure is the infinite tensor product of one-dimensional arbitrary Gaussian non-centered measures. We prove an irreducibility criterion for m = 3 m\!=\!3 . In 2019, the first author [28] established a criterion for m ≤ 2 m\!\leq\!2 . Our proof is in the same spirit, but the details are far more involved.



2008 MSC
22E65 , (28C20 , 43A80, 58D20)

1
Representations of the inductive limit of the general linear groups

{\rm GL}_{0}(2\infty,{\mathbb{R}})

1.1
Finite-dimensional case

Consider the space

X_{m,n}=\Big\{x=\sum_{1\leq k\leq m}\,\,\sum_{-n\leq r\leq n}x_{kr}E_{kr},\,\,x_{kr}\in{\mathbb{R}}\Big\},

where
E_{kn},\,\,k,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
are infinite matrix unities, with the measure (see (
1.5
))

\mu_{(b,a)}^{m,n}(x)=\otimes_{k=1}^{m}\otimes_{r=-n}^{n}\mu_{(b_{kr},a_{kr})}(x_{kr}).

Two groups act on the space
X_{m,n}
, namely
{\rm GL}(m,\mathbb{R})
from the left, and
\text{GL}(2n+1,\mathbb{R})
from the right, and their actions commute.
Therefore, two von Neumann algebras
{\mathfrak{A}}_{1,n}
and
{\mathfrak{A}}_{2,n}
in the Hilbert space
L^{2}(X_{m,n},\mu_{(b,a)}^{m,n})
generated
respectively by the left and the right actions of the corresponding groups have the property that
{\mathfrak{A}}_{1,n}^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathfrak{A}}_{2,n}
, where

{\mathfrak{A}}^{\prime}
is a commutant of a von Neuman algebra
{\mathfrak{A}}
.
We study what happens as
n\!\to\infty
. In the limit we obtain some unitary representation
T^{R,\mu,m}
(see (
1.6
)) of the group

G:={\rm GL}_{0}(2\infty,{\mathbb{R}})\!=\!\varinjlim_{n,i^{s}}{\rm GL}(2n+1,{\mathbb{R}})
acting from the right on
X_{m}
. In the generic case, the representation

T^{R,\mu,m}
is reducible. Indeed, if there exists a non-trivial element
s\in\text{GL}(m,\mathbb{R})
such the left action is
admissible
for
the measure
\mu_{(b,a)}^{m}
, i.e.,
(\mu_{(b,a)}^{m})^{L_{s}}\!\sim\!\mu_{(b,a)}^{m}
the operator
T^{L,\mu,m}_{s}
naturally associated with the left action,
is well defined
and
[T^{R,\mu,m}_{t},T^{L,\mu,m}_{s}]\!=\!0
for all
t\!\in\!G,\,s\!\in\!{\rm GL}(m,\mathbb{R})
.
We use notation
\mu^{f}(\Delta)=\mu\big(f^{-1}(\Delta)\big)
for
f:X\to X
, where
\Delta
is some measurable set in
X
.

The main result
of the article is the following.
The representation
T^{R,\mu,m}
is irreducible, see Theorem
1.1

if and only if
no left actions are admissible
, i.e.,
when
(\mu_{(b,a)}^{m})^{L_{s}}\perp\mu_{(b,a)}^{m}
for all
s\in\text{GL}(m,\mathbb{R})\backslash\{e\}
.
This is again a manifestation of the
Ismagilov conjecture
, see
[
27
]
.

Here, as in the case of the regular
[
18
,
19
]
and quasiregular
[
21
,
22
]
representations of the group
B^{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
,
which is an inductive limit of upper-triangular real matrices,
we obtain the remarkable result that the
irreducible representations
can be obtained as the
inductive limit of reducible representations
!

1.2
Infinite-dimensional case

Let us denote by
{\rm Mat}(2\infty,{\mathbb{R}})
the space of all
real matrices that are infinite in both directions:

{\rm Mat}(2\infty,{\mathbb{R}})=\Big\{x=\sum_{k,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}x_{kn}E_{kn},\,\,x_{kn}\in{\mathbb{R}}\Big\}.

(1.1)

The group
{\rm GL}_{0}(2\infty,{\mathbb{R}})=\varinjlim_{n,i^{s}}{\rm GL}(2n+1,{\mathbb{R}})
is defined as the inductive limit of the
general linear groups
G_{n}={\rm GL}(2n+1,{\mathbb{R}})
with
respect to the
symmetric embedding

i^{s}
:

G_{n}\ni x\mapsto i^{s}_{n+1}(x)=x+E_{-(n+1),-(n+1)}+E_{n+1,n+1}\in G_{n+1}.

(1.2)

For a fixed natural number
m
, consider a
G
-space
X_{m}
as the
following subspace of the space
{\rm Mat}(2\infty,{\mathbb{R}})
:

X_{m}=\Big\{x\in{\rm Mat}(2\infty,{\mathbb{R}})\mid x=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}x_{kn}E_{kn}\Big\}.

(1.3)

The group
{\rm GL}_{0}(2\infty,{\mathbb{R}})
acts from the right
on the space
X_{m}.
Namely, the right action of the group
{\rm GL}_{0}(2\infty,{\mathbb{R}})
is correctly defined on the space

X_{m}
by the formula
R_{t}(x)=xt^{-1},\,\,t\in G,\,\,x\in X_{m}
. We
define a Gaussian non-centered product measure

\mu:=\mu^{m}:=\mu_{(b,a)}^{m}

on the space
X_{m}:

\mu_{(b,a)}^{m}(x)=\otimes_{k=1}^{m}\otimes_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\mu_{(b_{kn},a_{kn})}(x_{kn}),

(1.4)

where

d\mu_{(b_{kn},a_{kn})}(x_{kn})=\sqrt{\frac{b_{kn}}{\pi}}e^{-b_{kn}(x_{kn}-a_{kn})^{2}}dx_{kn}

(1.5)

and
b=(b_{kn})_{k,n},\,\,b_{kn}>0,\,a=(a_{kn})_{k,n},\,a_{kn}\in{\mathbb{R}},\,1\leq k\leq m,\,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}.
Define the unitary
representation
T^{R,\mu,m}
of the group
{\rm GL}_{0}(2\infty,{\mathbb{R}})
on the space
L^{2}(X_{m},\mu^{m}_{(b,a)})

by the formula:

(T^{R,\mu,m}_{t}f)(x)=\big(d\mu_{(b,a)}^{m}(xt)/d\mu_{(b,a)}^{m}(x)\big)^{1/2}f(xt),\,\,f\in L^{2}(X_{m},\mu^{m}_{(b,a)}).

(1.6)

Obviously, the
centralizer

Z_{{\rm Aut}(X_{m})}(R(G))\subset{\rm Aut}(X_{m})
contains the group
L({\rm GL}(m,{\mathbb{R}}))
,
i.e., the image of the group
{\rm GL}(m,{\mathbb{R}})
with
respect to the left action
L:{\rm GL}(m,{\mathbb{R}})\rightarrow{\rm Aut}(X_{m}),\,L_{s}(x)\!=\!sx,\,s\in{\rm GL}(m,{\mathbb{R}}),\,x\in X_{m}.
We prove the following theorem.

Theorem 1.1
.

The representation
T^{R,\mu,m}\!:\!{\rm GL}_{0}(2\infty,{\mathbb{R}})\!\rightarrow\!U\Big(L^{2}(X_{m},\mu^{m}_{(b,a)})\Big)
is
irreducible, for
m=3
, if and only if

\displaystyle(i)

\displaystyle(\mu^{m}_{(b,a)})^{L_{s}}\perp\mu^{m}_{(b,a)}\quad\text{for all}\quad s\in{\rm GL}(m,{\mathbb{R}})\backslash\{e\};

\displaystyle(ii)

\displaystyle\text{the measure}\quad\mu^{m}_{(b,a)}\quad\text{is $G$-{\rm ergodic}}.

In
[
27
,
28
]

this result was proved for
m\leq 2
.
Note that conditions (i) and (ii) are necessary conditions for irreducibility.

Remark 1.1
.

Any Gaussian product-measure
\mu_{(b,a)}^{m}
on
X_{m}
is
{\rm GL}_{0}(2\infty,{\mathbb{R}})
-right-ergodic
[
34
, §3, Corollary 1]
, see Definition
2.1
. For non-product-measures
this is not true in general.

In order to study the condition
(\mu^{m}_{(b,a)})^{L_{t}}\perp\mu^{m}_{(b,a)}
for
t\in{\rm GL}(m,{\mathbb{R}})\setminus\{e\}
set

t=(t_{rs})_{r,s=1}^{m}\in{\rm GL}(m,{\mathbb{R}}),\,\,\,B_{n}={\rm diag}(b_{1n},b_{2n},...,b_{mn}),\,\,\,X_{n}(t)=B_{n}^{1/2}tB_{n}^{-1/2}.

(1.7)

Let
M^{i_{1}i_{2}...i_{r}}_{j_{1}j_{2}...j_{r}}(t)
be the
minors
of
the matrix
t
with
i_{1},i_{2},...,i_{r}
rows and
j_{1},j_{2},...,j_{r}

columns,
1\leq r\leq m.
Let
\delta_{rs}
be the Kronecker
symbols.

Lemma 1.2

(
[
27
]
, Lemma 10.2.3;
[
28
]
, Lemma 2.2)
.

For the measures
\mu_{(b,a)}^{m}
, with
m
a natural number,
the relation

(\mu_{(b,a)}^{m})^{L_{t}}\perp\mu_{(b,a)}^{m}\,\,\,\text{for all}\,\,\,t\in{\rm GL}(m,{\mathbb{R}})\backslash\{e\}\,\,

holds if and only if

\displaystyle\prod_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\frac{1}{2^{m}|{\rm det}\,\,t|}\,{\rm det}\left(I\!+\!X_{n}^{*}(t)X_{n}(t)\right)\!+\!\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\sum_{r=1}^{m}b_{rn}\Big(\!\sum_{s=1}^{m}(t_{rs}\!-\!\delta_{rs})a_{sn}\!\Big)^{2}\!=\!\infty,

where

\displaystyle{\rm det}\Big(I+X_{n}^{*}(t)X_{n}(t)\Big)=1+\sum_{r=1}^{m}\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}<...<i_{r}\leq m\atop 1\leq j_{1}<j_{2}<...<j_{r}\leq m}\left(M^{i_{1}i_{2}...i_{r}}_{j_{1}j_{2}...j_{r}}(X_{n}(t))\right)^{2}.

For the convenience of the reader this lemma is proved in Section
8.1
.

Remark 1.2
.

(The idea of the proof of
irreducibility.) Let us denote by
{\mathfrak{A}}^{m}
the von
Neumann algebra generated by the representation
T^{R,\mu,m}
, i.e.,
{\mathfrak{A}^{m}}=(T^{R,\mu,m}_{t}\mid t\in G)^{\prime\prime}.
For

\alpha\!=\!(\alpha_{k})\!\in\!\{0,1\}^{m}
define the von Neumann algebra

L^{\infty}_{\alpha}(X_{m},\mu^{m})
as follows:

L^{\infty}_{\alpha}(X_{m},\mu^{m})\!=\!\Big(\exp(itB^{\alpha}_{kn})\mid 1\leq k\leq m,\,\,t\in{\mathbb{R}},\,\,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}\Big)^{\prime\prime},\,\,

where
B^{\alpha}_{kn}\!=\!\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcc}x_{kn},&\text{if}&\alpha_{k}=0\\
i^{-1}D_{kn},&\text{if}&\alpha_{k}=1\end{array}\right.
and
D_{kn}=\partial/\partial x_{kn}-b_{kn}(x_{kn}-a_{kn})
.

The proof of the irreducibility is based on four
facts
:

1) we can approximate by generators

A_{kn}=A_{kn}^{R,m}=\frac{d}{dt}T^{R,\mu,m}_{I+tE_{kn}}|_{t=0}

the set of operators
(B^{\alpha}_{kn})_{k=1}^{m},\,n\!\in\!{\mathbb{Z}}
for some
\alpha\!\in\!\{0,1\}^{m}
depending on the measure

\mu^{m}
using the orthogonality condition

(\mu^{m})^{L_{s}}\perp\mu^{m}
for all
s\in{\rm GL}(m,{\mathbb{R}})\backslash{\{e\}}
,

2)
it is sufficient to verify the approximation only on the cyclic vector

{\bf 1}(x)\!\equiv\!1
, since the representation
T^{R,\mu,m}
is cyclic,

3) the subalgebra
L^{\infty}_{\alpha}(X_{m},\mu^{m})

is a maximal abelian subalgebra in
{\mathfrak{A}}^{m}
,

4) the measure
\mu^{m}
is
G
-ergodic.

Here the
generators

A_{kn}
are given by the formulas:

A_{kn}\!=\!\sum_{r=1}^{m}x_{rk}D_{rn},\quad k,n\in{\mathbb{Z}},\quad\text{where}\quad D_{kn}=\partial/\partial x_{kn}-b_{kn}(x_{kn}-a_{kn}).

Remark 1.3
.

Scheme of the proof. We prove the irreducibility as follows

\displaystyle\left(\mu^{L_{s}}\perp\mu\,\,\,\text{for all}\,\,\,s\in{\rm GL}(3,\mathbb{R})\setminus\{e\}\right)\Leftrightarrow\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\text{criteria}\\
\text{of}\\
\text{orthogonality}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\&

(1.8)

\displaystyle\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\text{Lemma~\ref{l.min=proj.3}}\\
\text{about}\\
\text{three vectors}\,f,g,h\not\in l_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\Rightarrow\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\text{some of}&\Delta^{(1)},&\Delta_{1}\\
\text{the expressions}&\Delta^{(2)},&\Delta_{2}\\
\text{are divergent:}&\Delta^{(3)},&\Delta_{3}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\Rightarrow\text{irreducibility},

\displaystyle\text{where}\quad\Delta^{(i)}:=\Delta(Y_{i}^{(i)},Y_{j}^{(i)},Y_{k}^{(i)}),\quad\Delta_{i}:=\Delta(Y_{i},Y_{j},Y_{k}),

(1.9)

\Delta(f,g,h)
is defined by (
8.15
),
and
\{i,j,k\}
is a cyclic permutation
of
\{1,2,3\}
, see for details
Lemmas
5.55
–
5.57
, Lemmas
5.52
–
5.54
and Lemmas
5.65
-
5.66
.

Remark 1.4
.

The fact that the conditions
(\mu^{3})^{L_{t}}\!\perp\!\mu^{3}
for all
t\in{\rm GL}(3,{\mathbb{R}})\backslash{\{e\}}
imply the possibility of the approximation of
x_{kn}
and
D_{kn}

by combinations of generators
is based on some
completely independent statement about three infinite vectors
f,g,h\not\in l_{2}

such that

C_{1}f+C_{2}g+C_{3}h\not\in l_{2}\quad\text{for arbitrary}\quad(C_{1},C_{2},C_{3})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\setminus\{0\},

(1.10)

see Lemma
8.83
for
m=2

[
28
]

and Lemma
8.87
for
m=3
.
A similar result for general
m
is studied in
[
29
]
.
These lemmas are the key ingredients
of the proof of the irreducibility of the representation.

Remark 1.5
.

Note that in the case of the “nilpotent group”
B_{0}^{\mathbb{N}}
and the infinite product of arbitrary Gaussian measures on
{\mathbb{R}}^{m}

(see
[
2
]
) the proof of the irreducibility is also based on another completely independent statement namely,
the Hadamard – Fischer inequality, see Lemma
1.3
.

Lemma 1.3

(Hadamard – Fischer inequality

[
11
,
12
]
)
.

For any
positive definite matrix
C\in{\rm Mat}(m,{\mathbb{R}}),\,\,m\in{\mathbb{N}}
and any two subsets
\alpha
and
\beta

with
\emptyset\subseteq\alpha,\,\beta\subseteq\{1,...,m\}
the
following inequality holds:

\left|\begin{array}[]{cc}M(\alpha)&M(\alpha\bigcap\beta)\\
M(\alpha\bigcup\beta)&M(\beta)\end{array}\right|=\left|\begin{array}[]{cc}A(\hat{\alpha})&A(\hat{\alpha}\bigcup\hat{\beta})\\
A(\hat{\alpha}\bigcap\hat{\beta})&A(\hat{\beta})\end{array}\right|\geq 0,

(1.11)

where
M(\alpha)=M^{\alpha}_{\alpha}(C),\,\,A(\alpha)=A^{\alpha}_{\alpha}(C)
and

\hat{\alpha}=\{1,...,m\}\setminus\alpha.

For the details see
[
11
, p.573]
and
[
12
, Chapter
2.5, problem 36]
.
In
[
2
]
the conditions of orthogonality
\mu^{L_{t}}\perp\mu
with respect to the left action of the group
B(m,{\mathbb{R}})
on
X^{m}
were expressed as the divergence of some series,
S^{L}_{kn}(\mu)=\infty,\,\,1\leq k<n\leq m
.
Conditions on the measure
\mu
for the variables
x_{kn}
to be approximated
by combinations of generators
A_{pq}
were expressed in terms of the divergence of another series
\Sigma_{kn}
.
The proof of the fact that conditions
S^{L}_{kn}(\mu)=\infty,\,\,1\leq\!k\!<\!n\!\leq m
imply the conditions

\Sigma_{kn}=\infty,\,\,1\leq k<n\leq m
was based on the
Hadamard – Fischer inequality.

2
Some orthogonality problem in measure theory

2.1
General setting

Our aim now is to find the minimal generating set of conditions of the orthogonality

(\mu^{m}_{(b,a)})^{L_{t}}\!\perp\!\mu^{m}_{(b,a)}
for all

t\in{\rm GL}(m,{\mathbb{R}})\setminus\{e\}
. To be more precise, consider the following more general situation.
Let
\alpha:G\rightarrow{\rm Aut}(X)
be a
measurable action
of a group
G
on a
measurable space
(X,\mu)
with the following property:

\mu^{\alpha_{t}}\perp\mu
for all
t\in G\setminus\{e\}
.
Define a
generating subset

G^{\perp}(\mu)
in the group
G

as follows:

\text{if}\quad\mu^{\alpha_{t}}\perp\mu\,\,\,\text{for all}\,\,\,t\in G^{\perp}(\mu),\quad\text{then}\quad\mu^{\alpha_{t}}\perp\mu\,\,\,\text{for all}\,\,\,t\in G\setminus\{e\}.

(2.1)

Problem 2.4
.

Find a
minimal generating subset

G^{\perp}_{0}(\mu)

satisfying
(
2.1
)
.

Definition 2.1
.

Recall that the probability measure
\mu
on some
G
-space
X
is called ergodic if any function

f\in L^{1}(X,\mu)
with property
f(\alpha_{t}(x))=f(x)\,\,{\rm mod}\,\mu
is constant.

2.2
Orthogonality criteria
\mu^{L_{t}}\perp\mu
for
t\in{\rm GL}(2,\mathbb{R})\setminus\{e\}

Remark 2.1
.

By Lemma 4.1 proved in
[
28
]
or Lemma 10.4.1 in
[
27
]
for
m=2
we conclude that
the minimal generating set
G^{\perp}_{0}(\mu)={\rm GL}(2,\mathbb{R})^{\perp}_{0}(\mu)
(see Problem (
2.4
))
is reduced to the following subgroups, families and elements:

\exp(tE_{12})=I+tE_{12}\!=\!\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&t\\
0&1\end{array}\right),\,\,\exp(tE_{21})\!=\!I+tE_{21}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
t&1\end{array}\right),

(2.2)

\exp(tE_{12})P_{1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-1&t\\
0&1\end{array}\right),\quad\exp(tE_{21})P_{2}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
t&-1\end{array}\right),

(2.3)

\tau_{-}(\phi,s)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\cos\phi&s^{2}\sin\phi\\
s^{-2}\sin\phi&-\cos\phi\end{array}\right)=D_{2}(s)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\cos\phi&-\sin\phi\\
\sin\phi&\cos\phi\end{array}\right)D^{-1}_{2}(s)P_{2}.

(2.4)

The families (
2.2
), (
2.3
) are one-parameter,
the family (
2.4
) is two-parameter.
All elements are of order
2
except the elements in subgroups given in (
2.2
)!
It suffices to verify the conditions (
2.2
) only for some
t\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}.

The family
\tau_{-}(\phi,s)
,
actually, coincides with

D_{2}(s){\rm O}(2)D^{-1}_{2}(s)P_{2}
, where
D_{2}(s)={\rm diag}(s,s^{-1})
.
All points
t
in (
2.3
) and all points
(\phi,s)
in (
2.4
) are essential, i.e., we can not remove any single point.

Remark 2.2
.

We note
[
16
, Chapter V, §8 Problems, 2, p. 147]
that every element of
{\rm SL}(2,{\mathbb{R}})
is
conjugate to at least one matrix of the form

\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}a&0\\
0&a^{-1}\end{array}\right),\,\,a\not=0,\,\,\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&t\\
0&1\end{array}\right),\,\,\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-1&t\\
0&-1\end{array}\right),\,\,\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\cos\phi&\sin\phi\\
-\sin\phi&\cos\phi\end{array}\right).

Remark 2.3
.

Some elements
a=gP_{r}
in the set
G^{\perp}_{0}(\mu)={\rm GL}(2,\mathbb{R})^{\perp}_{0}(\mu)
are of order
2

(see Remark
2.1
):

a^{2}=(gP_{r})^{2}=1.

(2.5)

This follows from the relation

P_{r}gP_{r}=g^{-1}.

(2.6)

To see this we note that
if (
2.6
) holds, then we get (
2.5
):

a^{2}=(gP_{r})^{2}=gP_{r}gP_{r}=gg^{-1}=1.

For example, for
g=\exp(tE_{12})
we get
P_{1}gP_{1}=g^{-1}
, for
g=\exp(tE_{21})
we get
P_{2}gP_{2}=g^{-1}

and for
g=\tau(\phi,s)
we get
P_{2}gP_{2}=g^{-1}
.
See (
2.3
) and (
2.4
) for details, where

\tau_{-}(\phi,s)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\cos\phi&s^{2}\sin\phi\\
s^{-2}\sin\phi&-\cos\phi\end{array}\right),\quad\tau(\phi,s)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\cos\phi&-s^{2}\sin\phi\\
s^{-2}\sin\phi&\cos\phi\end{array}\right).

We recall some useful lemmas from
[
28
]
.

Lemma 2.5
.

For
t=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}t_{11}&t_{12}\\
t_{21}&t_{22}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in{\rm GL}(2,{\mathbb{R}})\setminus\{e\}
we have, if
{\rm det}\,t>0
,

(\mu_{(b,0)}^{2})^{L_{t}}\perp\mu_{(b,0)}^{2}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad

\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\Big[(1-\mid{\rm det}\,t\mid)^{2}+(t_{11}-t_{22})^{2}+\Big(t_{12}\sqrt{\frac{b_{1n}}{b_{2n}}}+t_{21}\sqrt{\frac{b_{2n}}{b_{1n}}}\Big)^{2}\Big]=\infty.

(2.7)

If
{\rm det}\,t<0
we have

(\mu_{(b,0)}^{2})^{L_{t}}\perp\mu_{(b,0)}^{2}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad

\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\Big[(1-\mid{\rm det}\,t\mid)^{2}+(t_{11}+t_{22})^{2}+\Big(t_{12}\sqrt{\frac{b_{1n}}{b_{2n}}}-t_{21}\sqrt{\frac{b_{2n}}{b_{1n}}}\Big)^{2}\Big]=\infty.

(2.8)

Lemma 2.6
.

For
t\in{\rm GL}(2,{\mathbb{R}})\setminus\{e\}
we have

(\mu_{(b,a)}^{2})^{L_{t}}\perp\mu_{(b,a)}^{2}\quad{\it if}\quad|{\rm det}\,t|\not=1.

If
{\rm det}\,t=1
, we have

(\mu_{(b,a)}^{2})^{L_{t}}\perp\mu_{(b,a)}^{2}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\Sigma^{+}(t):=\Sigma_{1}^{+}(t)+\Sigma_{2}(t)=\infty.

If
{\rm det}\,t=-1
, we have

(\mu_{(b,a)}^{2})^{L_{t}}\perp\mu_{(b,a)}^{2}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\Sigma^{-}(t):=\Sigma_{1}^{-}(t)+\Sigma_{2}(t)=\infty,

where

\Sigma_{1}^{+}(t)=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\Big[(t_{11}-t_{22})^{2}+\Big(t_{12}\sqrt{\frac{b_{1n}}{b_{2n}}}+t_{21}\sqrt{\frac{b_{2n}}{b_{1n}}}\Big)^{2}\Big],

\Sigma_{1}^{-}(t)=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\Big[(t_{11}+t_{22})^{2}+\Big(t_{12}\sqrt{\frac{b_{1n}}{b_{2n}}}-t_{21}\sqrt{\frac{b_{2n}}{b_{1n}}}\Big)^{2}\Big],

\Sigma_{2}(t^{-1})=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\Big(b_{1n}\big[(t_{11}-1)a_{1n}+t_{12}a_{2n}\big]^{2}+b_{2n}\big[t_{21}a_{1n}+(t_{22}-1)a_{2n}\big]^{2}\Big).

(2.9)

Remark 2.4
.

By Lemma
2.6
we have

(\mu_{(b,a)}^{2})^{L_{t}}\perp\mu_{(b,a)}^{2}\quad\text{ for}\quad t\in{\rm GL}(2,{\mathbb{R}})\setminus\{e\}

if and only if this holds for two subsets
of the group

\pm{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{R})

defined as follows:

\displaystyle G^{+}_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{t\in{\rm SL}(2,{\mathbb{R}})\mid t_{11}=A^{1}_{1}(t)\},

(2.10)

\displaystyle G^{-}_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{t\in-{\rm SL}(2,{\mathbb{R}})\mid t_{11}=-A^{1}_{1}(t)\}.

(2.11)

The set
G^{+}_{2}

is reduced to two families of one-parameter subgroups (
2.2
).

(

, and two parameter family (

) of elements
from

.

Lemma 2.7
.

If
t\in G^{+}_{2}

If

\displaystyle\Sigma_{1}^{+}(t)=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\Big(t_{12}\sqrt{\frac{b_{1n}}{b_{2n}}}+t_{21}\sqrt{\frac{b_{2n}}{b_{1n}}}\Big)^{2},

(2.12)

\displaystyle\Sigma_{1}^{-}(t)=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\Big(t_{12}\sqrt{\frac{b_{1n}}{b_{2n}}}-t_{21}\sqrt{\frac{b_{2n}}{b_{1n}}}\Big)^{2}.

(2.13)

The conditions of

(2.14)

(2.15)

\Sigma^{-}_{12}\big(\tau_{-}(\phi,s)\big)=\sin^{2}\phi\,\Sigma_{1}(s)+\Sigma^{-}_{2}\big(\tau_{-}(\phi,s)\big),\,\,\phi\in[0,2\pi),\,\,s>0,

Recall Remark 4.3 from

.

Remark 2.5
.

The following three conditions are equivalent:

\displaystyle(i)

\displaystyle(ii)

\displaystyle\phi\in[0,2\pi),\,\,s>0,

\displaystyle(iii)

\displaystyle s\!>\!0,\,(C_{1},C_{2})\!\in\!{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\!\setminus\!\{0\},

where

) and

(2.19)

2.3
Equivalent series and equivalent sequences

There is an extensive
theory of convergent and divergent series. In our context we are only interested in when a series with positive coefficients is divergent or convergent.

Definition 2.2
.

We say that two series
\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}a_{n}
and
\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}b_{n}
with positive
a_{n},\,b_{n}
are equivalent if they are divergent or convergent simultaneously. We will write

\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}a_{n}\sim\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}b_{n}
.
We say that two sequences
(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(b_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
are equivalent if for some

C_{1},C_{2}>0
we have
C_{1}b_{n}\leq a_{n}\leq C_{2}b_{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. We will use the same notation
a_{n}\sim b_{n}
.

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
1+c_{n}>0
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then two series
are equivalent:

\Sigma_{1}:=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{c_{n}^{2}}{1+c_{n}},\quad\Sigma_{2}:=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}c_{n}^{2}.

(2.20)

Proof 1
.

Fix some
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and a big
N
. We have three cases:

(a)
1+c_{n}\in(\varepsilon,N)
,

(b) for infinite subset
\mathbb{Z}_{1}
we have
\lim_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{1}}c_{n}=\infty
,

(c) for infinite subset
\mathbb{Z}_{1}
we have
\lim_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{1}}(1+c_{n})=0
.

In the case (a) we have

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}c_{n}^{2}<\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{c_{n}^{2}}{1+c_{n}}<\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}c_{n}^{2},

(2.21)

In the case (b) and (c) both series are divergent.
∎

We will make systematic use of the following statement.

Remark 2.6

(
[
27
]
)
.

Let
a_{n},b_{n}>0
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. The following two series are equivalent

\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{a_{n}}{a_{n}+b_{n}}\sim\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{a_{n}}{b_{n}}.

(2.22)

2.4
Orthogonality criteria
\mu^{L_{t}}\perp\mu
for
t\in{\rm GL}(3,\mathbb{R})\setminus\{e\}

For
m=2
and
{\rm det}\,t>0
we have,
here
H_{m,n}(t)
is defined by (
8.8
)

\displaystyle 2^{2}\mid{\rm det}\,t\mid\big(H_{2,n}^{-2}(t)-1\big)\!=\!\Big[(1\!-\!\mid{\rm det}\,t\mid)^{2}\!+\!(t_{11}\!-\!t_{22})^{2}\!+\!\Big(t_{12}\sqrt{\frac{b_{1n}}{b_{2n}}}\!+\!t_{21}\sqrt{\frac{b_{2n}}{b_{1n}}}\Big)^{2}\Big]\!=

\displaystyle\left[\big(M^{\emptyset}_{\emptyset}(X(t))\!-\!A^{\emptyset}_{\emptyset}(X(t))\big)^{2}+\big(M^{1}_{1}(X(t))\!-\!A^{1}_{1}(X(t))\big)^{2}+\big(M^{1}_{2}(X(t))\!-\!A^{1}_{2}(X(t))\big)^{2}\right].

For
m=3
using (
1.7
) we have
X(t)=B^{1/2}tB^{-1/2}
, hence

X(t)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}b_{1n}&0&0\\
0&b_{2n}&0\\
0&0&b_{3n}\end{array}\right)^{1/2}\!\!\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}t_{11}&t_{12}&t_{13}\\
t_{21}&t_{22}&t_{23}\\
t_{31}&t_{32}&t_{33}\\
\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}b_{1n}&0&0\\
0&b_{2n}&0\\
0&0&b_{3n}\end{array}\right)^{-1/2}=\!\!

\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}t_{11}&\sqrt{\frac{b_{1n}}{b_{2n}}}t_{12}&\sqrt{\frac{b_{1n}}{b_{3n}}}t_{13}\\
\sqrt{\frac{b_{2n}}{b_{1n}}}t_{21}&t_{22}&\sqrt{\frac{b_{2n}}{b_{3n}}}t_{23}\\
\sqrt{\frac{b_{3n}}{b_{1n}}}t_{31}&\sqrt{\frac{b_{3n}}{b_{2n}}}t_{32}&t_{33}\end{array}\right).

Therefore, using
(
7.8
)
and the fact that
X=X^{*}(t)X(t)
we obtain

\displaystyle 2^{3}\mid{\rm det}\,t\mid H_{3,n}^{-2}(t)=\Big(1+\mid{\rm det}\,t\mid^{2}+t_{11}^{2}+\frac{b_{1n}}{b_{2n}}t_{12}^{2}+\frac{b_{1n}}{b_{3n}}t_{13}^{2}+\frac{b_{2n}}{b_{1n}}t_{21}^{2}

\displaystyle+t_{22}^{2}+\frac{b_{2n}}{b_{3n}}t_{23}^{2}+\frac{b_{3n}}{b_{1n}}t_{31}^{2}+\frac{b_{3n}}{b_{2n}}t_{32}^{2}+t_{33}^{2}+(M^{12}_{12}(t))^{2}+\frac{b_{2n}}{b_{3n}}(M^{12}_{13}(t))^{2}+

\displaystyle\frac{b_{1n}}{b_{3n}}(M^{12}_{23}(t))^{2}+\frac{b_{3n}}{b_{2n}}(M^{13}_{12}(t))^{2}+(M^{13}_{13}(t))^{2}+\frac{b_{1n}}{b_{2n}}(M^{13}_{23}(t))^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{b_{3n}}{b_{1n}}(M^{23}_{12}(t))^{2}+\frac{b_{2n}}{b_{2n}}(M^{23}_{13}(t))^{2}+(M^{23}_{23}(t))^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle=1+\mid{\rm det}\,t\mid^{2}+\sum_{1\leq i\leq j\leq 3}\Big[\left(t^{i}_{j}\sqrt{\frac{b_{in}}{b_{jn}}}\right)^{2}+\left(A^{i}_{j}\sqrt{\frac{b_{jn}}{b_{in}}}\right)^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle=1+\mid{\rm det}\,t\mid^{2}+\sum_{1\leq i\leq j\leq 3}\left(|M^{i}_{j}(X(t))|^{2}+|A^{i}_{j}(X(t))|^{2}\right).

Using the notation
t^{i}_{j}=t_{ij}
and the fact

{\rm det}\,t=t^{k}_{1}A^{k}_{1}+t^{k}_{2}A^{k}_{2}+t^{k}_{3}A^{k}_{3},\quad k=1,2,3,

we get

2^{3}\mid{\rm det}\,t\mid\big(H_{3,n}^{-2}(t)-1\big)=(1-\mid{\rm det}\,t\mid)^{2}+\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq 3}\Big(M^{i}_{j}(X(t))-A^{i}_{j}(X(t))\Big)^{2}

=(1-\mid{\rm det}\,t\mid)^{2}+\sum_{1\leq i\leq j\leq 3}\Big(t^{i}_{j}\sqrt{\frac{b_{in}}{b_{jn}}}-A^{i}_{j}(t)\sqrt{\frac{b_{jn}}{b_{in}}}\Big)^{2}.

(2.23)

Similar to
[

we get the following lemma, for

Proof 2
.

Consider the subset
{\rm GL}(3,{\mathbb{R}})^{\perp}_{0}(\mu)
of
{\rm GL}(3,{\mathbb{R}})
described by (
2.35
).
The fact that this set is minimal generating will follow from Lemma
4.29
, more precisely,
from the following implications:

\displaystyle\Big(\mu^{L_{t}}\perp\mu\,\,\,\text{for all}\,\,\,t\in{\rm GL}(3,{\mathbb{R}})^{\perp}_{0}(\mu)\Big)\Rightarrow\Big(\text{irreducibility}\Big)

(2.47)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\Big(\mu^{L_{t}}\perp\mu\,\,\,\text{for all}\,\,\,t\in{\rm GL}(3,{\mathbb{R}})\setminus\{e\}\Big).

The first implication is just Lemma
4.29
.
The second implication follows from irreducibility.
Indeed, suppose that
{\rm GL}(3,{\mathbb{R}})^{\perp}_{0}(\mu)
is not a minimal generating set. Then we can find an
s\in{\rm GL}(3,{\mathbb{R}})\setminus\{e\}
having the property

\big(\mu_{(b,a)}^{3}\big)^{L_{s}}\sim\mu_{(b,a)}^{3}.

Hence the non-trivial operator
T^{L,\mu,3}_{s}

can be defined by

(T^{L,\mu,3}_{s}f)(x)=\big(d\mu_{(b,a)}^{3}(s^{-1}x)/d\mu_{(b,a)}^{3}(x)\big)^{1/2}f(s^{-1}x),\,\,f\in L^{2}(X_{3},\mu^{3}_{(b,a)}).

(2.48)

This operator commutes with the representations
T^{R,\mu,3}
:

[T^{R,\mu,3}_{t},T^{L,\mu,3}_{s}]=0\quad\text{for all}\quad t\in G,

contradicting the irreducibility.

The relations (
2.43
)–(
2.46
)
follows from (
2.26
)–(
2.28
).
The relation (
2.45
), for example, follows from (
2.27
) and (
2.28
).
The relation (
2.50
)
we obtain from (
2.26
) for
\tau_{r}(t,s),\,\,t\!\in\!{\rm O}(3),\,\,s\!\in\!\big({\mathbb{R}^{*}}\big)^{3}

defined by

\tau_{r}(t,s)=D_{3}(s)tD_{3}^{-1}(s)P_{r},\quad\text{where}\quad D_{3}(s)={\rm diag}(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}).

(2.49)

∎

Lemma 2.13
.

Set

\tau(s,t):=D_{3}(s)tD_{3}^{-1}(s),\quad\tau_{r}(s,t):=\tau(s,t)P_{r}

for
t\!\in\!\pm{\rm O}(3),\,\,D_{3}(s)\!=\!{\rm diag}(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}),\,\,s\!=\!(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})\!\in\!(\mathbb{R}^{*})^{3}

\big(\mu_{(b,a)}^{3}\big)^{L_{\tau_{r}(s,t)}}\perp\mu_{(b,a)}^{3}\Leftrightarrow\Sigma_{1}^{\pm}\big(\tau_{r}(s,t)\big)+\Sigma_{2}\big(\tau_{r}(s,t)\big)=\infty,

where

2.32
) and

\Sigma_{2}(t)
is defined by (
2.28
).

In particular, if we denote
s_{ij}=s_{i}s_{j}^{-1}
we get

\Sigma_{1}^{+}\big(\tau(t,s)\big)=\Sigma_{1}^{+}(t,s)=t^{2}_{12}\Sigma_{12}(s_{12}^{1/2})+t^{2}_{13}\Sigma_{13}(s_{13}^{1/2})+t^{2}_{23}\Sigma_{23}(s_{23}^{1/2}).

(2.51)

Proof 3
.

For
T:=\tau(s,t)
and
T(3):=\tau_{3}(s,t)
we have respectively:

\displaystyle T=D_{3}(s)tD_{3}^{-1}(s)\!=\!\left(\!\begin{smallmatrix}t_{11}&\frac{s_{1}}{s_{2}}t_{12}&\frac{s_{1}}{s_{3}}t_{13}\\
\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}}t_{21}&t_{22}&\frac{s_{2}}{s_{3}}t_{23}\\
\frac{s_{3}}{s_{1}}t_{31}&\frac{s_{3}}{s_{2}}t_{32}&t_{33}\end{smallmatrix}\!\right),

(2.52)

\displaystyle\left(\begin{smallmatrix}t_{11}&\frac{s_{1}}{s_{2}}t_{12}&-\frac{s_{1}}{s_{3}}t_{13}\\
\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}}t_{21}&t_{22}&-\frac{s_{2}}{s_{3}}t_{23}\\
\frac{s_{3}}{s_{1}}t_{31}&\frac{s_{3}}{s_{2}}t_{32}&-t_{33}\end{smallmatrix}\right)=D_{3}(s)tD_{3}^{-1}(s)P_{3}=:T(3).

(2.53)

By Lemma
2.14
we have for
t\in{\rm O}(3)

t_{kr}=A^{k}_{r}(t),\,\,1\leq k,r\leq 3.

(2.54)

Therefore, for
T
and
T(3)
we have for
1\!\leq\!k,r\!\leq\!3
:

\displaystyle M^{k}_{r}(T)=T_{kr}\!=\!\frac{s_{k}}{s_{r}}t_{kr},\,\,A^{k}_{r}(T)\!=\!\frac{s_{r}}{s_{k}}A^{k}_{r}(t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{O(3)-M^i_j}}}{{=}}\frac{s_{r}}{s_{k}}t_{kr},\quad M^{k}_{r}(T(3))\quad\quad

(2.55)

\displaystyle\!=\!(-1)^{\delta_{3,r}}\frac{s_{k}}{s_{r}}t_{kr},\,\,A^{k}_{r}(T(3))\!=(-1)^{\delta_{3,r}}\frac{s_{r}}{s_{k}}A^{k}_{r}(t)=(-1)^{\delta_{3,r}}\frac{s_{r}}{s_{k}}t_{kr}.

(2.56)

Finally, we get

\displaystyle\Sigma_{1}^{+}(T)=\Sigma_{1}^{+}\big(\tau(s,t)\big)=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\Big[\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq 3}\Big(M^{i}_{j}(T)\sqrt{\frac{b_{in}}{b_{jn}}}-A^{i}_{j}(T)\sqrt{\frac{b_{jn}}{b_{in}}}\Big)^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle=\!\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\Big[t_{12}^{2}\Big(s_{12}\sqrt{\frac{b_{1n}}{b_{2n}}}\!-\!s_{12}^{-1}\sqrt{\frac{b_{2n}}{b_{1n}}}\Big)^{2}+t_{13}^{2}\Big(s_{13}\sqrt{\frac{b_{1n}}{b_{3n}}}\!-\!s_{13}^{-1}\sqrt{\frac{b_{3n}}{b_{1n}}}\Big)^{2}+

\displaystyle t_{23}^{2}\Big(s_{23}\sqrt{\frac{b_{2n}}{b_{3n}}}\!-\!s_{23}^{-1}\sqrt{\frac{b_{3n}}{b_{2n}}}\Big)^{2}\Big]=t^{2}_{12}\Sigma_{12}(s_{12}^{1/2})+t^{2}_{13}\Sigma_{13}(s_{13}^{1/2})+t^{2}_{23}\Sigma_{23}(s_{23}^{1/2}).

\Sigma_{1}^{-}(T(3))=t^{2}_{12}\Sigma_{12}(s_{12}^{1/2})+t^{2}_{13}\Sigma_{13}(s_{13}^{1/2})+t^{2}_{23}\Sigma_{23}(s_{23}^{1/2})

∎

Lemma 2.14
.

For an arbitrary orthogonal matrix
t\in\pm{\rm O}(3)

we have

t_{kn}=\pm A^{k}_{n}(t),\,\,1\leq k,n\leq 3,\quad\text{where}\quad t=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}t_{11}&t_{12}&t_{13}\\
t_{21}&t_{22}&t_{23}\\
t_{31}&t_{32}&t_{33}\end{smallmatrix}\right).

(2.57)

Proof 4
.

Denote the three rows of the matrix
t

by, respectively,
t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Since
t\in\pm{\rm O}(3)
we get

\|t_{1}\|^{2}=\|t_{2}\|^{2}=\|t_{3}\|^{2}=1\quad\text{and}\quad t_{l}\perp t_{r},\,\,l\not=r.

(2.58)

Moreover, since
t_{1}
is orthogonal to the hyperplane
V_{23}
generated by the vectors
t_{2}
and
t_{3}

and
t\in\pm{\rm O}(3)
we get respectively
t_{l}=\pm[t_{r},t_{s}]
, where

[x,y]
is the vector product or cross product
of two vectors
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
and
the triple
\{l,r,s\}

denotes any cyclic permutations of
\{1,2,3\}
.
For
t\in{\rm O}(3)
and
l=1
we get

t_{1}=[t_{2},t_{3}]=\left|\begin{smallmatrix}i&j&k\\
t_{21}&t_{22}&t_{23}\\
t_{31}&t_{32}&t_{33}\end{smallmatrix}\right|=i\left|\begin{smallmatrix}t_{22}&t_{23}\\
t_{32}&t_{33}\end{smallmatrix}\right|-j\left|\begin{smallmatrix}t_{21}&t_{23}\\
t_{31}&t_{33}\end{smallmatrix}\right|+k\left|\begin{smallmatrix}t_{21}&t_{22}\\
t_{31}&t_{32}\end{smallmatrix}\right|,

(2.59)

where
i,j,k
is the standard orthonormal basis in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, i.e.,

i=(1,0,0),\,\,\,j=(0,1,0),\,\,\,k=(0,0,1).

Define
X
formally as the matrix:

X=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}i&j&k\\
x_{1}&x_{2}&x_{3}\\
y_{1}&y_{2}&y_{3}\end{smallmatrix}\right).

Then

t_{1}=(t_{11},t_{12},t_{13})=\left(A^{1}_{1}(X),A^{1}_{2}(X),A^{1}_{3}(X)\right),

thus proving (
2.57
) for
k=1
. For other rows the proof is similar.
∎

Remark 2.9
.

For
t\in\pm{\rm O}(n)
we can prove a similar statement.

3
Irreducibility, the cases
m=1
and
m=2

For convenience of the reader, we recall the previous results (see details
in
[
28
]
).

3.1
Case
m=1

Let us denote by
\langle f_{n}\mid n\in{\mathbb{N}}\rangle
the
closure of the linear space

generated by the set of
vectors
(f_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
in a Hilbert space
H.
Consider the measure
\mu_{(b,a)}

on
the space
X_{1}\sim\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{Z}}=\otimes_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}
,
the infinite product of the real lines:

d\mu_{(b,a)}(x)=\otimes_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\sqrt{\frac{b_{1n}}{\pi}}e^{-b_{1n}(x_{1n}-a_{1n})^{2}}dx_{1n},

for
b=(b_{1n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
and
a=(a_{1n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
with
b_{1n}>0,\,\,a_{1n}\in\mathbb{R}
where
x=(x_{1n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
.
In the case
m=1
the generators
A_{kn}:=A_{kn}^{R,1}
have the form

A_{kn}=x_{1k}D_{1n},\quad k,n\in{\mathbb{Z}},

where
D_{kn}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{kn}}-b_{kn}(x_{kn}-a_{kn}).
The following lemmas are proved in
[
1
]

Lemma 3.15
.

The following three conditions are equivalent:

(i)
\,\,(\mu_{(b,a)})^{L_{t}}\perp\mu_{(b,a)}
for all
t\in{\rm GL}(1,{\mathbb{R}})\setminus\{e\}
,

(ii)
(\mu_{(b,a)})^{L_{-E_{11}}}\perp\mu_{(b,a)}
,

(iii)
S^{L}_{11}(\mu)=4\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{b_{1n}}a_{1n}^{2}=\infty.

Lemma 3.16
.

For
k,m\in{\mathbb{Z}}
we have

x_{1k}x_{1m}{\bf 1}\in\langle A_{kn}A_{mn}{\bf 1}=x_{1k}x_{1m}D_{1n}^{2}{\bf 1}\mid n\in{\mathbb{Z}}\rangle.

Lemma 3.17
.

For any
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
we have

x_{1k}{\bf 1}\in\langle x_{1k}x_{1n}{\bf 1}\mid n\in{\mathbb{Z}}\rangle\Leftrightarrow S^{L}_{11}(\mu)=\infty.

So, the operators
x_{1k},\,\,k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
are
affiliated

with the von Neumann algebra
{\mathfrak{A}}^{1}
generated by the representation, which completes
the proof of the irreducibility for
m=1.

Definition 3.1
.

Recall (see
[
8
]
) that, a not
necessarily bounded self-adjoint operator
A
in a Hilbert space

H
, is said to be affiliated with a von Neumann algebra
M

of operators in this Hilbert space
H
if
e^{itA}\in M
for all

t\in{\mathbb{R}}
. One writes
A\,\,\eta\,\,M
.

3.2
Case
m=2
, approximation of
x_{kn}
and
D_{kn}

In this case the generators

A_{kn}:=A_{kn}^{R,2}:=\frac{d}{dt}T^{R,\mu,2}_{I+tE_{kn}}\mid_{t=0}

have the form:

A_{kn}=x_{1k}D_{1n}+x_{2k}D_{2n},\quad k,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}.

We will formulate several
useful lemmas for
approximation of the operators of multiplication by the independent variables
x_{kn}
and operators

D_{kn}
by combinations of the generators
A_{kn}
.
In what follows we use the following notation for
f,g\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
(see Remark
2.6
for notations
\sim
)

\Delta(f,g):=\frac{\Gamma(f)+\Gamma(f,g)}{\Gamma(g)+1}\sim\frac{I+\Gamma(f)+\Gamma(g)+\Gamma(f,g)}{I+\Gamma(g)}=\frac{{\rm det}(I+\gamma(f,g))}{{\rm det}(I+\gamma(g))},

(3.1)

where
\Gamma(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
is the Gram determinant
of vectors
x_{1},x_{2},...,x_{n}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
defined by (
6.5
).
To make the notations of the article
[
28
]
compatible with the notations in the case
m=3
(see (
4.5
)), we denote

\displaystyle\|Y_{1}^{(1)}\|^{2}:=\!\|f^{1}\|^{2}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{b_{1k}^{2}}{b_{1k}^{2}+2b_{1k}b_{2k}},\hskip 9.24994pt\|Y_{2}^{(1)}\|^{2}:=\|g^{1}\|^{2}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{b_{2k}^{2}}{b_{1k}^{2}+2b_{1k}b_{2k}},

\displaystyle\|Y_{1}^{(2)}\|^{2}:=\!\|g^{2}\|^{2}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{b_{1k}^{2}}{b_{2k}^{2}+2b_{1k}b_{2k}},\hskip 9.24994pt\|Y_{2}^{(2)}\|^{2}:=\!\|f^{2}\|^{2}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{b_{2k}^{2}}{b_{2k}^{2}+2b_{1k}b_{2k}},

\displaystyle\|Y_{1}\|^{2}:=\|f\|^{2}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{a_{1k}^{2}}{\frac{1}{2b_{1k}}+\frac{1}{2b_{2k}}},\hskip 9.24994pt\|Y_{2}\|^{2}:=\|g\|^{2}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{a_{2k}^{2}}{\frac{1}{2b_{1k}}+\frac{1}{2b_{2k}}}.

(3.2)

Lemma 3.18
.

For any
k,t\in{\mathbb{Z}}
one has

x_{1k}x_{1t}\in\langle A_{kn}A_{tn}{\bf 1}\mid n\in{\mathbb{Z}}\rangle\,\,\Leftrightarrow\,\,\Delta(Y_{1}^{(1)},Y_{2}^{(1)}).

Lemma 3.19
.

For any
k,t\in{\mathbb{Z}}
we have

x_{2k}x_{2t}\in\langle A_{kn}A_{tn}{\bf 1}\mid n\in{\mathbb{Z}}\rangle\,\,\Leftrightarrow\,\,\Delta(Y_{2}^{(2)},Y_{1}^{(2)})=\infty.)

Lemma 3.20
.

Set
\Sigma^{rs}=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\frac{b_{rn}}{b_{sn}},\,\,1\leq r,s\leq 2
. For any
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
we get

x_{1k}{\bf 1}\in\langle D_{1n}A_{kn}{\bf 1}\mid n\in{\mathbb{Z}}\rangle\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\Sigma^{12}=\infty.

Lemma 3.21
.

For any
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
we have

x_{2k}{\bf 1}\in\langle D_{2n}A_{kn}{\bf 1}\mid n\in{\mathbb{Z}}\rangle\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\Sigma^{21}=\infty.

Lemma 3.22
.

For any
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
we have

D_{1n}{\bf 1}\in\langle A_{kn}{\bf 1}\mid k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\rangle\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\Delta(Y_{1},Y_{2})=\infty.

Lemma 3.23
.

For any
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
we have

D_{2n}{\bf 1}\in\langle A_{kn}{\bf 1}\mid k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\rangle\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\Delta(Y_{2},Y_{1})=\infty.

3.2.1
Technical part of the proof of irreducibility

Lemma 3.24
.

If
\mu^{L_{t}}\perp\mu
for all
t\in{\rm GL}(2,{\mathbb{R}})\setminus\{e\}
, we can approximate
by combinations of generators
at least one of
the following pairs of operators:

(x_{1n},x_{2n}),\,\,(x_{1n},D_{2n}),\,\,(D_{1n},x_{2n})\,\,
or

(D_{1n},D_{2n})
.

Proof 5
.

Recall the orthogonality conditions for the case
m=2

\displaystyle S^{L}_{kr}(\mu)=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\frac{b_{kn}}{2}\left(\frac{1}{2b_{rn}}+a_{rn}^{2}\right),\quad 1\leq k,r\leq 2,\,\,k\not=r,

\displaystyle S^{L,-}_{kr}(\mu,t)=\frac{t^{2}}{4}\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\frac{b_{kn}}{b_{rn}}+\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\frac{b_{kn}}{2}(-2a_{kn}+ta_{rn})^{2},\quad 1\leq k\not=r\leq 2,

\displaystyle\Sigma^{-}_{12}\big(\tau_{-}(\phi,s)\big)=\sin^{2}\phi\,\Sigma_{1}(s)+\Sigma^{-}_{2}\big(\tau_{-}(\phi,s)\big),\quad\text{where}

\displaystyle\Sigma_{1}(s):=\!\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\Big(s^{2}\sqrt{\frac{b_{1n}}{b_{2n}}}\!-\!s^{-2}\sqrt{\frac{b_{2n}}{b_{1n}}}\Big)^{2},

\displaystyle\Sigma^{-}_{2}\big(\tau_{-}(\phi,s)\big)\!=\!\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\Big(4b_{1n}\sin^{2}\frac{\phi}{2}\!+\!4s^{-4}b_{2n}\cos^{2}\frac{\phi}{2}\Big)\Big(a_{1n}\sin\frac{\phi}{2}\!-\!s^{2}a_{2n}\cos\frac{\phi}{2}\Big)^{2}.

Let
{\mathfrak{A}}^{2}
be the von Neumann algebra generated by our representation.
In order to approximate
operators
x_{kn}
or
D_{kn}
by the corresponding generators,
by Lemmas
3.18
–
3.19
, Lemmas
3.16
–
3.16
and Lemmas
3.22
–
3.23
we have:

\displaystyle x_{1n}x_{1t}\,\,\eta\,\,{\mathfrak{A}}^{2}\Leftrightarrow\Delta(Y_{1}^{(1)},Y_{2}^{(1)})=\infty,\quad x_{2n}x_{2t}\,\,\eta\,\,{\mathfrak{A}}^{2}\Leftrightarrow\Delta(Y_{2}^{(2)},Y_{1}^{(2)})=\infty,

\displaystyle D_{1n}\,\,\eta\,\,{\mathfrak{A}}^{2}\Leftrightarrow\Delta(Y_{1},Y_{2})=\infty,\quad D_{2n}\,\,\eta\,\,{\mathfrak{A}}^{2}\Leftrightarrow\Delta(Y_{2},Y_{1})=\infty,

where
Y_{r}^{(s)}
and
Y_{r}
for
1\leq r,s\leq 2
are defined by (
3.2
).
∎

3.2.2
Scheme of the proof for two lines

There are two different cases:

{\rm I.}

Approximation of
x_{rk}x_{rt}
for
1\leq r\leq 2
by
A_{kn}A_{tn}
,

{\rm II.}

Approximation of
D_{rk}
for
1\leq r\leq 2
by
A_{kn}
.

In the case
m=2
the analysis of the divergence

\Delta(Y_{1}^{(1)},Y_{2}^{(1)})=\infty\quad\text{and}\quad\Delta(Y_{2}^{(2)},Y_{1}^{(2)})=\infty

is governed by the
convergence or divergence
of

\Sigma^{12}
and
\Sigma^{21}
, since

\displaystyle\|Y_{1}^{(1)}\|^{2}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{b_{1k}^{2}}{b_{1k}^{2}+2b_{1k}b_{2k}}\sim\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{b_{1k}^{2}}{b_{1k}b_{2k}}=\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{12},

\displaystyle\|Y_{2}^{(2)}\|^{2}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{b_{2k}^{2}}{b_{2k}^{2}+2b_{1k}b_{2k}}\sim\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{b_{2k}^{2}}{b_{1k}b_{2k}}=\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{21}.

Remark 3.1
.

To guess the right generalisation for the case
m=3
we note that
\Sigma^{12}=S_{1}(2)
and
\Sigma^{21}=S_{2}(2)
, where we denote

\displaystyle S_{1}(2):=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{b_{1k}^{2}}{b_{1k}b_{2k}},\,\,\,S_{2}(2):=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{b_{2k}^{2}}{b_{1k}b_{2k}},\,\,\,\Sigma^{12}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{b_{1k}}{b_{2k}},\,\,\,\Sigma^{21}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{b_{2k}}{b_{1k}}.

We also observe that

\|Y_{1}^{(1)}\|^{2}\sim S_{1}(2),\quad\|Y_{2}^{(2)}\|^{2}\sim S_{2}(2).

(3.3)

Hence, in the case
m=3
it is natural to replace
\Sigma^{12}=S_{1}(2)
and
\Sigma^{21}=S_{2}(2)
by

S_{r}(3)
, which is defined as follows:

S_{r}(3)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{b_{rn}^{2}}{b_{1n}b_{2n}+b_{1n}b_{3n}+b_{2n}b_{3n}},\quad 1\leq r\leq 3.

(3.4)

3.2.3
I, Approximation of
x_{1n}
and
x_{2n}

Lemma 3.25
.

We have

\displaystyle S_{1}(2)+S_{2}(2)=\infty,

(3.5)

\displaystyle\|Y_{r}^{(r)}\|^{2}\sim S_{r}(2)\quad{for\,\,\,all}\quad 1\leq r\leq 2,

(3.6)

\displaystyle\|Y_{1}^{(2)}\|^{2}<\frac{1}{2}S_{1}(2),\quad\|Y_{2}^{(1)}\|^{2}<\frac{1}{2}S_{2}(2),

(3.7)

\displaystyle\|Y_{1}^{(i)}\|^{2}+\|Y_{2}^{(j)}\|^{2}=\infty,\quad i,j\in\{1,2\}.

(3.8)

Proof 6
.

Since
a^{2}+b^{2}\geq 2ab
we get

S_{1}(2)+S_{2}(2)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{b_{1k}^{2}+b_{2k}^{2}}{b_{1k}b_{2k}}\geq\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}2=\infty.

Further, for
1\leq r\leq 2

\displaystyle\|Y_{r}^{(r)}\|^{2}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{b_{rk}^{2}}{b_{rk}^{2}+2b_{1k}b_{2k}}\sim\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{b_{rk}^{2}}{2b_{1k}b_{2k}}=\frac{1}{2}S_{r}(2),

\displaystyle\|Y_{1}^{(2)}\|^{2}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{b_{1k}^{2}}{b_{2k}^{2}+2b_{1k}b_{2k}}<\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{b_{1k}^{2}}{2b_{1k}b_{2k}}=\frac{1}{2}S_{1}(2),

\displaystyle\|Y_{2}^{(1)}\|^{2}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{b_{2k}^{2}}{b_{1k}^{2}+2b_{1k}b_{2k}}<\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{b_{2k}^{2}}{2b_{1k}b_{2k}}=\frac{1}{2}S_{2}(2).

In addition, we have

\displaystyle\|Y_{1}^{(i)}\|^{2}+\|Y_{2}^{(j)}\|^{2}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{b_{1k}^{2}}{b_{ik}^{2}+2b_{1k}b_{2k}}+\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{b_{2k}^{2}}{b_{jk}^{2}+2b_{1k}b_{2k}}

\displaystyle>\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{b_{1k}^{2}+b_{2k}^{2}}{(b_{1k}+b_{2k})^{2}}=\infty.\hskip 236.15796pt\Box

Remark 3.2
.

In what follows if some expression
<\infty
(resp.
=\infty
)
we denote this case by
0
(respectively, by
1
).

Set
S=(S_{1}(2),S_{2}(2))
, since
S_{1}(2)+S_{2}(2)=\infty
we have two cases:

I(1)
S=(0,1)
,
if
S=(1,0)
we interchange
(b_{1n},a_{1n})_{n}
with
(b_{2n},a_{2n})_{n}
,

I(2)
S=(1,1)
,
i.e.,
S_{1}(2)=\infty,\,\,S_{2}(2)=\infty
.

3.2.4
Case
S=(0,1)

Lemma 3.26
.

In the case
S=(0,1)
the representation is irreducible, moreover we can approximate:

(1)

x_{2k}x_{2t}
by
A_{kn}A_{tn}
, since
\Delta(Y_{2}^{(2)},Y_{1}^{(2)})=\infty
,

(2)

D_{1n},\,D_{2n}
by
A_{kn}
, since
\Delta(Y_{1},Y_{2})=\infty
and
\Delta(Y_{2},Y_{1})=\infty
.

Proof 7
.

(1) Set (compare with (
4.12
) in the case
m=3
)

\displaystyle y^{(k)}=(y_{1}^{(k)},y_{2}^{(k)})=(\|Y_{1}^{(k)}\|^{2},\|Y_{2}^{(k)}\|^{2}),\,\,1\leq k\leq 2,

(3.9)

\displaystyle\,\,\text{or}\,\,y=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}y^{(1)}\\
y^{(2)}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}y_{1}^{(1)}&y_{2}^{(1)}\\
y_{1}^{(2)}&y_{2}^{(2)}\end{array}\right).

In the case
S=(0,1)
we have by Lemma
3.25
(see Remark
3.2
)

y^{(1)}=(0,1),\,\,y^{(2)}=(0,1)\,\,\text{or}\,\,\left(\begin{array}[]{c}y^{(1)}\\
y^{(2)}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
0&1\end{array}\right).

(3.15)

Indeed,
\|Y_{1}^{(1)}\|^{2}\sim S_{1}(2)<\infty
, hence
\|Y_{2}^{(1)}\|^{2}=\infty
by (
3.8
).
Further,
\Gamma(Y_{2}^{(2)})=\|Y_{2}^{(2)}\|^{2}\sim S_{2}(2)=\infty
, and
\Gamma(Y_{1}^{(2)})<\infty
, since

S_{1}(2)<\infty
. Therefore,

\Delta(Y_{2}^{(2)},Y_{1}^{(2)})=\frac{\Gamma(Y_{2}^{(2)})+\Gamma(Y_{2}^{(2)},Y_{1}^{(2)})}{1+\Gamma(Y_{1}^{(2)})}>\frac{\Gamma(Y_{2}^{(2)})}{1+\Gamma(Y_{1}^{(2)})}=\infty,

and we conclude that
x_{2n}\,\,\eta\,\,{\mathfrak{A}}^{2}
.

(2) Further, since

\|Y_{1}\|^{2}=\|Y_{2}\|^{2}=\|Y_{1}-sY_{2}\|^{2}=\infty

(3.16)

by Lemma
8.83
, we conclude that
\Delta(Y_{1},Y_{2})=\Delta(Y_{2},Y_{1})=\infty
,
so
D_{1n},\,D_{2n}\,\,\eta\,\,{\mathfrak{A}}^{2}
by Lemmas
3.22
and
3.23
.
Finally,
x_{2n},\,D_{1n},\,D_{2n}\,\eta\,\,{\mathfrak{A}}^{2}
. Now we get

A_{kn}-x_{2k}D_{2n}=x_{1k}D_{1n},\quad k,n\in{\mathbb{Z}},

and the proof is complete since
we are in the case
m=1
.

Relations (
3.16
) follows from

S_{1}(2)\!=\!\Sigma^{12}\!=\!\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{b_{1k}}{b_{2k}}<\infty
. Indeed, we have

\|Y_{1}\|^{2}=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\frac{a_{1k}^{2}}{\frac{1}{2b_{1k}}+\frac{1}{2b_{2k}}}=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\frac{b_{1k}a_{1k}^{2}}{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{b_{1k}}{2b_{2k}}}\sim 2\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}b_{1k}a_{1k}^{2}=S^{L}_{11}(\mu)=\infty,

\|Y_{2}\|^{2}=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\frac{b_{1k}a_{2k}^{2}}{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{b_{1k}}{2b_{2k}}}\sim\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}b_{1k}a_{2k}^{2}\sim\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\frac{b_{1k}}{2}\Big(\frac{1}{2b_{2k}}+a_{2k}^{2}\Big)=S^{L}_{12}(\mu)=\infty,

\|Y_{1}-sY_{2}\|^{2}\!=\!\!\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\frac{b_{1k}(a_{1k}\!-\!sa_{2k})^{2}}{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{b_{1k}}{2b_{2k}}}\!\sim\!\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}b_{1k}(a_{1k}\!-\!sa_{2k})^{2}\!=\!\frac{1}{4}\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}b_{1k}(-2a_{1k}+2sa_{2k})^{2}

\sim\frac{1}{2}\Big[\frac{(2s)^{2}}{4}\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\frac{b_{1k}}{b_{2k}}+\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\frac{b_{1k}}{2}(-2a_{1k}+2sa_{2k})^{2}\Big]=\frac{1}{2}S^{L,-}_{12}(\mu,t)=\infty,

for
t=2s
.
∎

3.2.5
Case
S=(1,1)

In the case
S=(1,1)
we have three possibilities (see Remark

)

{\rm I(2a)}\,\,y=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\

(3.17)

i.e., I(2a)
y_{2}^{(1)}<\infty
and

,

).

In the first case I(2a) or in the second case I(2b), i.e., if

, we conclude

x_{2n}
.

It remains to consider the case I(2c):
\|Y_{2}^{(1)}\|^{2}=\|Y_{1}^{(2)}\|^{2}=\infty
.
In the case
S=(1,1)
set
c_{n}=\frac{b_{2n}}{b_{1n}},\,\,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

We get

\displaystyle\|Y_{1}^{(1)}\|^{2}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{1+2c_{n}}\sim\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{c_{n}},\quad\|Y_{2}^{(1)}\|^{2}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{c_{n}^{2}}{1+2c_{n}},

(3.18)

\displaystyle\|Y_{1}^{(2)}\|^{2}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{c_{n}^{2}+2c_{n}},\quad\|Y_{2}^{(2)}\|^{2}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{c_{n}^{2}}{c_{n}^{2}+2c_{n}}\sim\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}c_{n}.

(3.19)

Remark 3.3
.

We show that if
\|Y_{2}\|^{2}<\infty
and

S^{L}_{12}(\mu)=\infty
, then
\sum_{n}\frac{b_{1n}}{b_{2n}}=\infty
.
Indeed, let us suppose that
\sum_{n}\frac{b_{1n}}{b_{2n}}<\infty
,
then

\|Y_{2}\|^{2}=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\frac{a_{2n}^{2}}{\frac{1}{2b_{1n}}+\frac{1}{2b_{2n}}}\sim\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}b_{1n}a_{2n}^{2}\sim\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\frac{b_{1n}}{2}\Big(\frac{1}{2b_{2n}}+a_{2n}^{2}\Big)=S_{12}^{L}(\mu)=\infty.

(3.23)

Lemma 3.27
.

If

for any

, then

\|Y_{1}^{(1)}-CY_{2}^{(1)}\|^{2}=\infty\quad\text{and}\quad\|Y_{2}^{(2)}-CY_{1}^{(2)}\|^{2}=\infty,\quad\text{for any}\quad C\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}.

.

Lemma 3.28
.

When

\Sigma_{12}(s)<\infty
and

,
we get

\sigma(C_{1},C_{2}):=\|C_{1}Y_{1}+C_{2}Y_{2}\|^{2}\!=\!\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\frac{(C_{1}a_{1n}+C_{2}a_{2n})^{2}}{\frac{1}{2b_{1n}}+\frac{1}{2b_{2n}}}\!=\!\infty,\,\,(C_{1},C_{2})\!\in\!{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\setminus\{0\}.

(3.32)

. Therefore, by Lemma

we have

Thus the proof of Lemma
3.24
for
m=2
is completed.

The proof of the irreducibility for
m=2
follows from
Remark
1.2
. Depending on the measure, we can
approximate four different families of commuting operators

B^{\alpha}=(B^{\alpha}_{1n},B^{\alpha}_{2n})_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
for

\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{2}
:

B^{(0,0)}\!\!=\!\!(x_{1n},x_{2n})_{n},\,\,B^{(0,1)}\!\!=\!\!(x_{1n},D_{2n})_{n},\,\,B^{(1,0)}\!\!=\!\!(D_{1n},x_{2n})_{n},\,\,B^{(0,0)}\!\!=\!\!(D_{1n},D_{2n})_{n}.

The von Neumann algebra
L^{\infty}_{\alpha}(X_{2},\mu^{2})
consists of
all essentially bounded functions
f(B^{\alpha})
in the commuting
family of operators
B^{\alpha}
(see, e.g.,
[
5
]
) as, in
particular,
L^{\infty}_{(0,0)}(X_{2},\mu^{2})=L^{\infty}(X_{2},\mu^{2})
.
Since the von Neumann algebras
L^{\infty}_{\alpha}(X_{2},\mu^{2})
are
maximal abelian, the commutant
\big({\mathfrak{A}}^{2}\big)^{\prime}
of the
von Neumann algebra
{\mathfrak{A}}^{2}
generated by the
representation is contained in
L^{\infty}_{\alpha}(X_{2},\mu^{2})
.
Hence, the bounded operator
A\in\big({\mathfrak{A}}^{2}\big)^{\prime}
will
be some function
A=a(B^{\alpha})\in L^{\infty}_{\alpha}(X_{2},\mu^{2})
.
The commutation relation
[A,T^{R,\mu,2}_{t}]=0
gives us the
following relations:

a(B^{\alpha})^{R_{t}}=a(B^{\alpha})
for all
t\in{\rm GL}_{0}(2\infty,{\mathbb{R}}).

Set
B^{\alpha}_{r}=(B^{\alpha}_{rn})_{n},\,\,x_{r}=(x_{rn})_{n},\,\,D_{r}=(x_{rn})_{n},\,\,r=1,2,\,\,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and set as before,
E_{kn}(t):=I+tE_{kn},\,\,t\in{\mathbb{R}},\,\,k,n\in{\mathbb{Z}},\,\,k\not=n
. Then the action
(B^{\alpha})^{R_{t}}
is
defined as follows:

\displaystyle(B^{\alpha}_{1},B^{\alpha}_{2})^{R_{t}}=((B^{\alpha}_{1})^{R_{t}},(B^{\alpha}_{2})^{R_{t}}),\quad(x_{r})^{R_{t}}=x_{r}t,\quad(D_{r})^{R_{t}}=D_{r}t^{T},

\displaystyle a(\dots,x_{rk},\dots,x_{rn},\dots)^{R_{E_{kn}(t)}}:=a(\dots,x_{rk},\dots,x_{rn}+tx_{rk},\dots),

\displaystyle a(\dots,D_{rk},\dots,D_{rn},\dots)^{R_{E_{kn}(t)}}:=a(\dots,D_{rk}+tD_{rn},\dots,D_{rn},\dots),\,\,\,t\in{\mathbb{R}}.

In all the cases, by ergodicity of the measure
\mu^{2}
, we conclude that
a
is constant.

4
Irreducibility, the case
m=3

4.1
Technical part of the proof of irreducibility

Lemma 4.29
.

If
\mu^{L_{t}}\perp\mu
for all
t\in{\rm GL}(3,{\mathbb{R}})\setminus\{e\}
, we can approximate at least one of the following eight triplets of operators:

\displaystyle(x_{1n},x_{2n},x_{3n}),\,\,(x_{1n},x_{2n},D_{3n}),\,\,(x_{1n},D_{2n},x_{3n}),\,\,(D_{1n},x_{2n},x_{3n}),

\displaystyle(x_{1n},D_{2n},D_{3n}),(D_{1n},x_{2n},D_{3n}),\,\,(D_{1n},D_{2n},x_{3n}),\,\,(D_{1n},D_{2n},D_{3n}).

Proof 8
.

By Lemma
2.11
,
the condition of orthogonality
(\mu_{(b,a)}^{3})^{L_{t}}\perp\mu_{(b,a)}^{3}

for
t\in\pm{\rm SL}(3,\mathbb{R})\setminus\{e\}
are,

\Sigma^{\pm}(t)=\Sigma_{1}^{\pm}(t)+\Sigma_{2}(t)=\infty,

(4.1)

where
\Sigma_{2}(t)
is defined by (
2.28
) and

\Sigma_{1}^{+}(t),\,\,\Sigma_{1}^{-}(t)
are defined by (
2.31
), (
2.32
).
Let
{\mathfrak{A}}^{3}
be the von Neumann algebra generated by the representation.
We write compactly:

\displaystyle x_{kn}\,\,\eta\,\,{\mathfrak{A}}^{3}\Leftrightarrow\Delta^{(k)}=\infty,\quad D_{kn}\,\,\eta\,\,{\mathfrak{A}}^{3}\Leftrightarrow\Delta_{k}=\infty,

(4.2)

\displaystyle\text{where}\,\,\Delta^{(k)}:=\Delta(Y_{k}^{(k)},Y_{r}^{(k)},Y_{s}^{(k)}),\quad\Delta_{k}:=\Delta(Y_{k},Y_{r},Y_{s}),

(4.3)

and
\{k,r,s\}
is a cyclic permutation of
\{1,2,3\}
.

Case I.
Approximation of
x_{rk}x_{rt}
for
1\leq r\leq 3
by
A_{kn}A_{tn}
.

Set
B_{3k}=b_{1k}+b_{2k}+b_{3k}
. To approximate the operators
x_{kn}
by the corresponding operators,
by Lemmas
5.52
–
5.54
we get:

x_{1n}x_{1t}\,\eta\,{\mathfrak{A}}^{3}\Leftrightarrow\,\Delta^{(1)}=\infty,\,\,x_{2n}x_{2t}\,\,\eta\,\,{\mathfrak{A}}^{3}\Leftrightarrow\Delta^{(2)}=\infty,\,\,x_{3n}x_{3t}\,\eta\,{\mathfrak{A}}^{3}\Leftrightarrow\Delta^{(3)}=\infty,

(4.4)

where

\|Y_{s}^{(r)}\|^{2}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{b_{rk}^{2}}{B_{3k}^{2}-(b_{1k}^{2}+b_{2k}^{2}+b_{3k}^{2}-b_{sk}^{2})},\quad 1\leq r,s\leq 3.

(4.5)

Case II.
Approximation of
D_{rn}
by
A_{kn}
.

By Lemmas
5.55
–
5.56
we have for
1\leq r\leq 3
(see (
4.3
)):

D_{rn}\,\,\eta\,\,{\mathfrak{A}}^{3}\Leftrightarrow\Delta_{r}=\infty,\quad\text{where}\quad\|Y_{r}\|^{2}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{a_{rk}^{2}}{\frac{1}{2b_{1k}}+\frac{1}{2b_{2k}}+\frac{1}{2b_{3k}}}.

(4.6)

Case III.
Approximation of
x_{rk}
by
D_{rn}A_{kn}
.

\displaystyle D_{1n}A_{kn}=x_{1k}D_{1n}^{2}+x_{2k}D_{1n}D_{2n}+x_{3k}D_{1n}D_{3n},

\displaystyle D_{2n}A_{kn}=x_{1k}D_{1n}D_{2n}+x_{2k}D_{2n}^{2}+x_{3k}D_{2n}D_{3n},

\displaystyle D_{3n}A_{kn}=x_{1k}D_{1n}D_{3n}+x_{2k}D_{2n}D_{3n}+x_{3k}D_{3n}^{2}.

By Lemmas
5.58
–
5.60
we have

\displaystyle x_{1n}{\bf 1}\in\langle D_{1n}A_{kn}{\bf 1}\mid k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\rangle\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\Sigma_{1}=\infty,

\displaystyle x_{2n}{\bf 1}\in\langle D_{2n}A_{kn}{\bf 1}\mid k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\rangle\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\Sigma_{2}=\infty,

\displaystyle x_{3n}{\bf 1}\in\langle D_{3n}A_{kn}{\bf 1}\mid k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\rangle\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\Sigma_{3}=\infty,

where
\Sigma_{r}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{b_{rk}}{b_{1k}+b_{2k}+b_{3k}}.

Case IV.
Approximation of
D_{rn}
by
x_{rk}A_{kn}
.

\displaystyle x_{1k}A_{kn}=x^{2}_{1k}D_{1n}+x_{1k}x_{2k}D_{2n}+x_{1k}x_{3k}D_{3n},

\displaystyle x_{2k}A_{kn}=x_{1k}x_{2k}D_{1n}+x_{2k}^{2}D_{2n}+x_{2k}x_{3k}D_{3n},

\displaystyle x_{3k}A_{kn}=x_{1k}x_{3k}D_{1n}+x_{2k}x_{3k}D_{2n}+x_{3k}^{2}D_{3n}.

By Lemmas
5.61
–
5.63
we have

\displaystyle D_{1n}{\bf 1}\in\langle x_{1k}A_{kn}{\bf 1}\mid k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\rangle\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\Delta(Y_{11},Y_{12},Y_{13})=\infty,

\displaystyle D_{2n}{\bf 1}\in\langle x_{2k}A_{kn}{\bf 1}\mid k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\rangle\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\Delta(Y_{22},Y_{23},Y_{21})=\infty,

\displaystyle D_{3n}{\bf 1}\in\langle x_{3k}A_{kn}{\bf 1}\mid k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\rangle\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\Delta(Y_{33},Y_{31},Y_{32})=\infty,

where
Y_{kr}
for
1\leq k,r\leq 3
are defined by
(
5.13
)–(
5.20
).

Case V.
By Lemma
2.11
we have two conditions

\displaystyle(A)\quad\Sigma_{1}^{+}(t)=\infty,\,\,\,{\rm det}\,t=1\quad\text{or}\quad\Sigma_{1}^{-}(t)=\infty,\,\,\,{\rm det}\,t=-1,

\displaystyle(B)\quad\Sigma_{1}^{+}(t)<\infty\quad\text{or}\quad\Sigma_{1}^{-}(t)<\infty\quad\text{but}\quad\Sigma_{2}(t)=\infty,

(4.7)

where
\Sigma_{1}^{+}(t),\,\,\Sigma_{1}^{-}(t),\quad\Sigma_{2}(t)
are defined respectively by
(
2.31
), (
2.32
)
and (
2.28
).
The rest
of this section will be devoted to the proof of Lemma
4.29
.
∎

4.1.1
Notations and the change of the variables

In what follows we will systematically use the following notations:

\displaystyle S_{r}(3)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{b_{rn}^{2}}{b_{1n}b_{2n}+b_{1n}b_{3n}+b_{2n}b_{3n}},\quad 1\leq r\leq 3,

(4.8)

\displaystyle\Sigma_{r}:=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{b_{rn}}{b_{1n}+b_{2n}+b_{3n}},\quad 1\leq r\leq 3,

(4.9)

\displaystyle\Sigma^{rs}:=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\frac{b_{rk}}{b_{sk}},\quad 1\leq r\not=s\leq 3,\quad C_{k}=\frac{1}{2b_{1k}}+\frac{1}{2b_{2k}}+\frac{1}{2b_{3k}},

(4.10)

\displaystyle y_{123}=(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}),\quad\text{where}\quad y_{r}:=\|Y_{r}\|^{2},

(4.11)

\displaystyle y^{(k)}\!=\!(y_{1}^{(k)},y_{2}^{(k)},y_{3}^{(k)})\!=\!(\|Y_{1}^{(k)}\|^{2},\|Y_{2}^{(k)}\|^{2},\|Y_{3}^{(k)}\|^{2}),\,1\leq k\leq 3,

(4.12)

\displaystyle y=\!\left(\begin{array}[]{c}y^{(1)}\\
y^{(2)}\\
y^{(3)}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}y_{1}^{(1)}&y_{2}^{(1)}&y_{3}^{(1)}\\
y_{1}^{(2)}&y_{2}^{(2)}&y_{3}^{(2)}\\
y_{1}^{(3)}&y_{2}^{(3)}&y_{3}^{(3)}\\
\end{array}\right),\,\,\text{where}\,\,y_{s}^{(r)}:=\|Y_{s}^{(r)}\|^{2},\,\,\,

\displaystyle\Sigma_{123}(s)=(\Sigma_{12}(s_{12}),\Sigma_{23}(s_{23}),\Sigma_{13}(s_{13})),\quad s=(s_{12},s_{23},s_{13}).

(4.20)

The expressions
S_{r}(3)
in the case
m=3
can be generalized for an arbitrary
m\in\mathbb{N}
as follows:

S_{k}(m)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{b_{kn}^{2}}{\sum_{1\leq r<s\leq m}b_{rn}b_{sn}},\quad 1\leq k\leq m.

(4.21)

Lemma 4.30
.

We have

\displaystyle S_{1}(3)+S_{2}(3)+S_{3}(3)=\infty,

(4.22)

\displaystyle\|Y_{r}^{(r)}\|^{2}\sim S_{r}(3)\quad{for\,\,\,all}\quad 1\leq r\leq 3,

(4.23)

\displaystyle\|Y_{r}^{(s)}\|^{2}<\frac{1}{2}S_{r}(3)\quad{for\,\,\,all}\quad 1\leq r\not=s\leq 3,

(4.24)

\displaystyle\|Y_{1}^{(i_{1})}\|^{2}+\|Y_{2}^{(i_{2})}\|^{2}+\|Y_{3}^{(i_{3})}\|^{2}=\infty,\quad i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}\in\{1,2,3\}.

(4.25)

Proof 9
.

Since
3(a^{2}+b^{2}+c^{2})\geq 2(ab+ac+bc)
we get

S_{1}(3)+S_{2}(3)+S_{3}(3)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{b_{1n}^{2}+b_{2n}^{2}+b_{3n}^{2}}{b_{1n}b_{2n}+b_{1n}b_{3n}+b_{2n}b_{3n}}\geq\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}2/3=\infty.

Further by (
4.5
)

\displaystyle\|Y_{r}^{(r)}\|^{2}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{b_{rn}^{2}}{b_{rn}^{2}+2(b_{1n}b_{2n}+b_{1n}b_{3n}+b_{2n}b_{3n})}\sim S_{r}(3),

\displaystyle\|Y_{r}^{(s)}\|^{2}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{b_{rn}^{2}}{b_{sn}^{2}+2(b_{1n}b_{2n}+b_{1n}b_{3n}+b_{2n}b_{3n})}<\frac{1}{2}S_{r}(3),\,\,s\not=r.

To prove (
4.25
) we get by (
4.5
)

\displaystyle\|Y_{1}^{(i_{1})}\|^{2}+\|Y_{2}^{(i_{2})}\|^{2}+\|Y_{3}^{(i_{3})}\|^{2}=

\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{3}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{b_{rn}^{2}}{b_{i_{r}n}^{2}+2(b_{1n}b_{2n}+b_{1n}b_{3n}+b_{2n}b_{3n})}>\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{\sum_{r=1}^{3}b_{rn}^{2}}{(\sum_{r=1}^{3}b_{rn})^{2}}=\infty.\hskip 65.44142pt\Box

We make the following change of the variables:

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}b_{1n}&b_{2n}&b_{3n}\\
a_{1n}&a_{2n}&a_{3n}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\to\left(\begin{smallmatrix}b_{1n}^{\prime}&b_{2n}^{\prime}&b_{3n}^{\prime}\\
a_{1n}^{\prime}&a_{2n}^{\prime}&a_{3n}^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&d_{2n}:=\frac{b_{2n}}{b_{1n}}&d_{3n}:=\frac{b_{3n}}{b_{1n}}\\
a_{1n}\sqrt{b_{1n}}&a_{2n}\sqrt{b_{1n}}&a_{3n}\sqrt{b_{1n}}\end{smallmatrix}\right),

(4.26)

motivated by
the following formulas:

\displaystyle d\mu_{(b,a)}(x)\!=\!\sqrt{\frac{b}{\pi}}\exp(-b(x\!-\!a)^{2})dx\!=\!\sqrt{\frac{1}{\pi}}\exp(-(x^{\prime}\!-\!a^{\prime})^{2})dx^{\prime}\!=\!d\mu_{(b^{\prime},a^{\prime})}(x^{\prime}),

\displaystyle d\mu_{(b_{2},a_{2})}(x)=\sqrt{\frac{b_{2}}{\pi}}\exp(-b_{2}(x\!-\!a_{2})^{2})dx=\sqrt{\frac{b_{2}}{b_{1}\pi}}\exp\Big(-\frac{b_{2}}{b_{1}}(x^{\prime}\!-\!a_{2}^{\prime})^{2}\Big)dx^{\prime}

\displaystyle=d\mu_{(b_{2}^{\prime},a_{2}^{\prime})}(x^{\prime}),\quad(b^{\prime},a^{\prime})=(1,a\sqrt{b}),\quad(b_{2}^{\prime},a_{2}^{\prime})=\Big(\frac{b_{2}}{b_{1}},a_{2}\sqrt{b_{1}}\Big).

Remark 4.1
.

All the expressions, given in the list (
2.26
) (
2.27
), (
2.28
) and (
4.1
)
are invariant under the transformations (
4.26
)

S^{L}_{kr}(\mu)=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\frac{b_{kn}}{2}\left(\frac{1}{2b_{rn}}+a_{rn}^{2}\right),\quad Y_{r}=\Big(a_{rk}\Big(\frac{1}{2b_{1k}}+\frac{1}{2b_{2k}}+\frac{1}{2b_{3k}}\Big)^{-1/2}\Big)_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}},

etc., and
S_{r}(3)
that are defined by (
3.4
).

4.2
Approximation scheme

Case I.

Approximation of
x_{kt}
by
A_{kn}A_{tn}
.

Recall that we will write
1
if some values
=\infty
and
0
in the case
<\infty
(see Remark
3.2
).
We use the following notation
S:=(S_{1}(3),S_{2}(3),S_{3}(3))
.
By Lemma
4.30
we get

\sum_{r=1}^{3}S_{r}(3)=\infty.

Therefore, without loss of generality, it suffices to consider the following
three cases:

(1)\,\,S=(0,0,1),\quad(2)\,\,S=(0,1,1),\quad(3)\,\,S=(1,1,1).

(4.27)

By Lemma
2.11
,
the condition of orthogonality
(\mu_{(b,a)}^{3})^{L_{t}}\perp\mu_{(b,a)}^{3}

for
t\in\pm{\rm SL}(3,\mathbb{R})\setminus\{e\}
, i.e.,

\Sigma^{\pm}(t)=\Sigma_{1}^{\pm}(t)+\Sigma_{2}(t)=\infty,

splits into two cases:

\begin{array}[]{cccc}(A)&\Sigma_{1}^{\pm}(t)=\infty,&&\Sigma_{1}^{\pm}(t)=\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq 3}\Sigma^{\pm}_{ij}(t),\\
(B)&\Sigma_{1}^{\pm}(t)<\infty&\text{ but}&\Sigma_{2}(t)=\infty,\end{array}

(4.28)

where
\Sigma_{1}^{\pm}(t),\,

\Sigma^{\pm}_{ij}(t)
and
\Sigma_{2}(t)
are defined by (
2.31
),
(
2.32
) , (
2.33
) and (
2.28
).

4.3
Case
S=(0,0,1)

Lemma 4.31
.

The case
S=(0,0,1)
is equivalent with

\Sigma^{13}+\Sigma^{23}<\infty,\quad S_{3}(3)\sim\sum_{n}\frac{b_{3n}^{2}}{b_{1n}b_{2n}}=\infty.

(4.29)

Proof 10
.

To prove the first part of (
4.29
)
we set
c_{n}=\frac{b_{3n}}{b_{1n}+b_{2n}}
and note that

\displaystyle\infty>S_{1}(3)+S_{2}(3)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{b^{2}_{1n}+b^{2}_{2n}}{b_{1n}b_{2n}+b_{1n}b_{3n}+b_{2n}b_{3n}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{two-sim-ser}}}{{\sim}}

\displaystyle\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{b_{1n}^{2}+b_{2n}^{2}}{(b_{1n}+b_{2n}+b_{3n})^{2}-b_{3n}^{2}}\sim\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{(b_{1n}+b_{2n})^{2}}{(b_{1n}+b_{2n}+b_{3n})^{2}-b_{3n}^{2}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{(1+c_{n})^{2}-c_{n}^{2}}\!=\!\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{1+2c_{n}}\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{two-sim-ser}}}{{\sim}}\!\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{c_{n}}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{b_{1n}+b_{2n}}{b_{3n}}=\Sigma^{13}+\Sigma^{23}.

To prove the second part of (
4.29
) we have by the first part of (
4.29
)

S_{3}(3)\!=\!\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{b^{2}_{3n}}{b_{1n}b_{2n}\!+\!b_{1n}b_{3n}\!+\!b_{2n}b_{3n}}\!=\!\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{\frac{b_{1n}b_{2n}}{b^{2}_{3n}}+\frac{b_{1n}}{b_{3n}}+\frac{b_{2n}}{b_{3n}}}\!\sim\!\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{b^{2}_{3n}}{b_{1n}b_{2n}}.\,\,\,\Box

In the case
S=(0,0,1)
we have

\Delta(Y_{3}^{(3)},Y_{1}^{(3)},Y_{2}^{(3)})\sim\Delta(Y_{3}^{(3)})\sim\|Y_{3}^{(3)}\|^{2}=\infty,

so we can approximate
x_{3n}x_{3t}
using Lemma
5.54
and after that we can approximate
x_{3n}
by an analogue of Lemma
3.17
.

From now on we will say that we can approximate
x_{3n}
using Lemma
5.54
, without mentioning Lemma
3.17
.

We can not approximate
x_{1n}
and
x_{2n}
using Lemma
5.52
-
5.53
, since we have

\Delta(Y_{1}^{(1)},Y_{2}^{(1)},Y_{3}^{(1)})+\Delta(Y_{2}^{(2)},Y_{3}^{(2)},Y_{1}^{(2)})<\infty.

We can try to approximate some of
D_{rn}
for
1\leq r\leq 3
using Lemmas
5.55
–
5.57
, see Section
4.4.4
for details.
We have for
1\leq k\leq 3
(see (
4.3
)):

D_{kn}\,\,\eta\,\,{\mathfrak{A}}^{3}\Leftrightarrow\Delta_{k}=\infty,\quad\text{where}\quad\Delta_{k}:=\Delta(Y_{k},Y_{r},Y_{s}),

and
\{k,r,s\}
is a cyclic permutation of
\{1,2,3\}
.
Recall that by
\Sigma^{12}+\Sigma^{13}<\infty
we get (see (
4.6
) for the expressions of
\|Y_{r}\|^{2},\,\,1\leq r\leq 3
)

\|Y_{1}\|^{2}\sim\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}b_{1n}a_{1n}^{2},\quad\|Y_{2}\|^{2}\sim\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}b_{1n}a_{2n}^{2},\quad\|Y_{3}\|^{2}\sim\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}b_{1n}a_{3n}^{2}.

(4.30)

By (
4.29
)
we have
\Sigma^{13}+\Sigma^{23}<\infty
. We distinguish two cases:

(1)
\Sigma^{12}<\infty
,

(2)
\Sigma^{12}=\infty
.

In the case (1), since
\Sigma^{12}+\Sigma^{13}<\infty
we have

\displaystyle S^{L}_{1,23}(\mu,t,s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{S^{L}_{1,23}}}}{{=}}\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\left[\frac{t^{2}}{4}\frac{b_{1n}}{b_{2n}}+\frac{s^{2}}{4}\frac{b_{1n}}{b_{3n}}+\frac{b_{1n}}{2}\big(\!-\!2a_{1n}\!+\!ta_{2n}\!+\!sa_{3n}\big)^{2}\right]\sim

\displaystyle\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\frac{b_{1n}}{2}\big(\!-\!2a_{1n}\!+\!ta_{2n}\!+\!sa_{3n}\big)^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{Y(r)-(001)}}}{{\sim}}\|C_{1}Y_{1}+\!C_{2}Y_{2}\!+\!C_{3}Y_{3}\|^{2}.

Finally, in the case (1) we can approximate all
D_{rn},\,1\leq r\leq 3
using Lemmas
5.55
–
5.57
and
Lemma
8.87

and the proof is finished.

The case (2)
can be divided into three
cases (if necessary, we can chose an appropriate subsequence of
\Big(\frac{b_{1n}}{b_{2n}}\Big)_{n}
)
:

\lim_{n}\frac{b_{1n}}{b_{2n}}=\!\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}(a)&0\\
(b)&b>0\\
(c)&\infty\end{array}\right..

(4.31)

The case (c) is reduced to the case (a) by exchanging
(b_{2n},a_{2n})
with
(b_{1n},a_{1n})
.

This transfomation does not change the first condition in (
4.29
).
In the case (2.a-b), by (
4.6
) we obtain the following expressions for
\|Y_{r}\|^{2},\,\,1\leq r\leq 3
:

\displaystyle\|Y_{1}\|^{2}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{a_{1n}^{2}}{\frac{1}{2b_{1n}}+\frac{1}{2b_{2n}}+\frac{1}{2b_{3n}}}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{2b_{1n}a_{1n}^{2}}{1+\frac{b_{1n}}{b_{2n}}+\frac{b_{1n}}{b_{3n}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma^{13}<\infty}}{{\sim}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}2b_{1n}a_{1n}^{2},

\displaystyle\|Y_{2}\|^{2}\sim\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}2b_{1n}a_{2n}^{2},\quad\|Y_{3}\|^{2}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}b_{1n}a_{3n}^{2}.

Since

\|Y_{1}\|^{2}\sim\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}b_{1n}a_{1n}^{2}\sim S^{L}_{11}(\mu)=\infty,

we have four possibilities
for
y_{23}:=(y_{2},y_{3})\in\{0,1\}^{2}
as in (
4.60
), see Section
4.4.4
:

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}&(1.0)&(1.1)&(1.2)&(1.3)\\
y_{1}&1&1&1&1\\
y_{2}&0&1&0&1\\
y_{3}&0&0&1&1\\
\end{array}

We just follow the instructions given in Remark
4.4
.
We note that the cases (1.0) and (1.1) can not occur since the following conditions are contradictory:

S^{L}_{13}(\mu)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{S^L(kn)}}}{{=}}\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\frac{b_{1n}}{2}\Big(\frac{1}{2b_{3n}}+a_{3n}^{2}\Big)\!=\!\infty,\,\,\,\|Y_{3}\|^{2}\sim\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}b_{1n}a_{3n}^{2}<\infty,\,\,\,\Sigma^{13}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{S(31)}}}{{<}}\infty.

We have two cases (1.2.1) and (1.3.1) according to whether respectively the expressions in
(
4.64
)
or (
4.66
) are
divergent. We can approximate in these cases respectively

D_{1n}
and
D_{3n}
in (
4.62
) and all
D_{1n},\,D_{2n},\,D_{3n}
in (
4.63
). The proof of irreducibilty is finished in both cases
because we have

x_{3n},\,D_{3n}\,\,\eta\,\,{\mathfrak{A}}^{3}

and the problem is reduced to the case
m=2

[
28
]
, since

A_{kn}\!=\!\sum_{r=1}^{3}x_{rk}D_{rn}-x_{3k}D_{3n}=\sum_{r=1}^{2}x_{rk}D_{rn}.

If the opposite holds,
we have two different cases (1.2.0) and (1.3.0).
We try
to approximate
D_{3n}
using Lemma
5.66
. If one of the expressions
\Sigma_{3}(D,s)
or
\Sigma^{\vee}_{3}(D,s)
is divergent
for some sequence
s=(s_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
,
we can approximate
D_{3k}
and the proof is finished, since we have
x_{3n},\,D_{3n}\,\,\eta\,\,{\mathfrak{A}}^{3}

and the problem is reduced to the case
m=2
.
Let us suppose, as in Remark
4.6
, that for every sequence
s=(s_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
we have

\Sigma_{3}(D,s)+\Sigma^{\vee}_{3}(D,s)<\infty.

Then, in particular, we have for
s^{(3)}=(s_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}

with
\frac{s^{2}_{k}}{b_{3k}}\equiv 1

\displaystyle\infty>\Sigma_{3}(D,s^{(3)})+\Sigma^{\vee}_{3}(D,s^{(3)})\sim\Sigma_{3}(D)+\Sigma^{\vee}_{3}(D)=

\displaystyle\sum_{k}\frac{\frac{1}{2b_{3k}}+a^{2}_{3k}}{C_{k}+a^{2}_{1k}+a^{2}_{2k}+a^{2}_{3k}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{two-sim-ser}}}{{\sim}}\sum_{k}\frac{\frac{1}{2b_{3k}}+a^{2}_{3k}}{\frac{1}{2b_{1k}}+a^{2}_{1k}+\frac{1}{2b_{2k}}+a^{2}_{2k}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{k}\frac{\frac{b_{1k}}{b_{3k}}+2b_{1k}a^{2}_{3k}}{1+2b_{1k}a^{2}_{1k}+\frac{b_{1k}}{b_{2k}}+2b_{1k}a^{2}_{2k}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{S(31)}}}{{\sim}}\sum_{k}\frac{2b_{1k}a^{2}_{3k}}{1+2b_{1k}a^{2}_{1k}+2b_{1k}a^{2}_{2k}}=:\Sigma^{+}_{3}(D).

Remark 4.2
.

Finally, we have
\Sigma^{+}_{3}(D)\!\sim\!\sum_{k}\frac{2a^{2}_{3k}}{1+2a^{2}_{1k}+2a^{2}_{2k}}
, we take
b_{1n}\!\equiv\!1
by (
4.26
).
In the case (1.2.0) we have

\|Y_{2}\|^{2}\sim\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}b_{1n}a_{2n}^{2}<\infty
, and therefore

\Sigma^{+}_{3}(D)\sim\sum_{k}\frac{2a^{2}_{3k}}{1+2a^{2}_{1k}}
, and hence

\Sigma^{+}_{3}(D)=\infty
by Lemma
4.38
.
In the case (1.3.0) we have
a_{3}=\pm a_{1}\pm a_{2}+h
or
a_{3}-h=\pm a_{1}\pm a_{2}
,
see the proof of Lemma
4.39
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\infty>\Sigma^{+}_{3}(D)\sim\sum_{k}\frac{a^{2}_{3k}}{1+a^{2}_{1k}+a^{2}_{2k}}\geq\sum_{k}\frac{a^{2}_{3k}}{1+a^{2}_{1k}+2|a_{1k}||a_{2k}|+a^{2}_{2k}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{k}\frac{a^{2}_{3k}}{1+\big(|a_{1k}|+|a_{2k}|\big)^{2}},\quad\infty>\Sigma^{+}_{3}(D)\sim\sum_{k}\frac{a^{2}_{3k}}{1+a^{2}_{1k}+a^{2}_{2k}}\geq

(4.32)

\displaystyle\sum_{k}\frac{a^{2}_{3k}}{1+a^{2}_{1k}+a^{2}_{2k}+(|a_{1k}|-|a_{2k}|)^{2}}\sim\sum_{k}\frac{a^{2}_{3k}}{1+2a^{2}_{1k}-2|a_{1k}||a_{2k}|+2a^{2}_{2k}}

\displaystyle\sim\sum_{k}\frac{a^{2}_{3k}}{1+a^{2}_{1k}-2|a_{1k}||a_{2k}|+a^{2}_{2k}}\sim\sum_{k}\frac{a^{2}_{3k}}{1+\big(|a_{1k}|-|a_{2k}|\big)^{2}}.

(4.33)

Hence, we have by (
4.32
) and (
4.33
)

\infty>\Sigma^{+}_{3}(D)\geq\sum_{k}\frac{a^{2}_{3k}}{1+\big(\pm a_{1k}\pm a_{2k}\big)^{2}}=\sum_{k}\frac{a^{2}_{3k}}{1+\big(a_{3k}-h_{k}\big)^{2}}=\infty

(4.34)

by Lemma
4.38
, contradiction.
Therefore, in both cases we can approximate
D_{3n}
and the proof is finished.

4.4
Case
S=(0,1,1)

Lemma 4.32
.

In the case
S=(0,1,1)

we have

\lim_{n}d_{2n}=\lim_{n}d_{3n}=\infty.

(4.35)

Proof 11

(4.36)

\displaystyle\sim\!\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{d_{2n}^{2}}{(1\!+\!d_{2n})(d_{2n}\!+\!d_{3n})}\sim\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{d_{2n}^{2}}{d_{2n}\!+\!d_{3n}}\!\leq\!\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{C^{2}}{C\!+\!d_{3n}}\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{two-sim-ser}}}{{\sim}}\!\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{d_{3n}}\!<\!\infty,

a contradiction.
We use the fact that for any fixed

the function

is strictly increasing when

.
Similarly, if we suppose that

∎

Lemma 4.33
.

The case

4.4.1
Approximation of
x_{2n},\,x_{3n}

To approximate
x_{2n},\,x_{3n}
, we need several lemmas.
Denote
l_{n}=d_{n}^{-1}
.

Lemma 4.36
.

The following five series are equivalent:

\displaystyle(i-ii)\quad\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{\big(C_{2}\!-\!C_{3}d_{n}\big)^{2}}{1+2d_{n}}\sim\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}c_{n}^{2},

(4.51)

\displaystyle(iii-iv)\quad\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{\big(C_{2}l_{n}\!-\!C_{3}\big)^{2}}{1+2l_{n}}\sim\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}e_{n}^{2},

(4.52)

\displaystyle(v)\,\,\Sigma_{23}(s)\!=\!\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\Big(s^{2}\sqrt{\frac{b_{2n}}{b_{3n}}}\!-\!s^{-2}\sqrt{\frac{b_{3n}}{b_{2n}}}\Big)^{2}\!=\!\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\Big(\frac{s^{2}}{\sqrt{d_{n}}}\!-\!\frac{\sqrt{d_{n}}}{s^{2}}\Big)^{2},\quad

(4.53)

where

d_{n}\!=\!C_{2}C_{3}^{-1}(1+c_{n}),\,\,\,l_{n}\!=\!C_{3}C_{2}^{-1}(1+e_{n}),\,\,s^{4}\!=\!C_{2}C_{3}^{-1}>0,\quad l_{n}\!=\!d_{n}^{-1}.

(4.54)

Proof 15
.

To prove (
4.51
) and (
4.52
)
we get by Lemma
2.8
using (
4.54
)

\displaystyle\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{\big(C_{2}\!-\!C_{3}d_{n}\big)^{2}}{1+2d_{n}}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{C_{2}^{2}c_{n}^{2}}{1+2C_{2}C_{3}^{-1}(1+c_{n})}\sim\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}c_{n}^{2},

\displaystyle\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{\big(C_{2}l_{n}\!-\!C_{3}\big)^{2}}{1+2l_{n}}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{C_{3}^{2}e_{n}^{2}}{1+2C_{3}C_{2}^{-1}(1+e_{n})}\sim\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}e_{n}^{2}.

To finish the proof we make use of the following lemma
∎

Lemma 4.37
.

Let
(c_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
be a sequence of real numbers
with
1\!+c_{n}\!>\!0
and
(1+c_{n})(1+e_{n})=1
.
Then the following three series are equivalent:

\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\left((1+e_{n})^{1/2}-(1+e_{n})^{-1/2}\right)^{2},\quad\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}c_{n}^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}e_{n}^{2}.

Proof 16
.

Set
s^{4}=C_{2}C_{3}^{-1}
, replacing
1+c_{n}
by
(1+e_{n})^{-1}

in Lemma
8.74
gives

\Sigma_{23}(s)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\left((1+c_{n})^{-1/2}-(1+c_{n})^{1/2}\right)^{2}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\left((1+e_{n})^{1/2}-(1+e_{n})^{-1/2}\right)^{2}.

Therefore,
\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{c_{n}^{2}}{1\!+\!c_{n}}\!=\!\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{e_{n}^{2}}{1\!+\!e_{n}}
and hence, by Lemma
2.8
, the two series are equivalent:
\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}c_{n}^{2}\!\sim\!\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}e_{n}^{2}
.
∎

4.4.2
Two remaining possibilities

By Lemma
4.36
there are only two cases:

(1) when
\rho_{2}(C_{2},C_{3})=\rho_{3}(C_{2},C_{3})=\infty
for all
(C_{2},C_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}
,

(2) when both
\rho_{2}(C_{2},C_{3})
and
\rho_{3}(C_{2},C_{3})
are finite and hence,
\Sigma_{23}(s)<\infty
.
To illustrate this we start with the following example

Example 4.1
.

Set
d_{n}=n^{\alpha}
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
with
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
. We have

\lim_{n}d_{n}=\!\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcc}\infty&\text{if}&\alpha>0\\
1&\text{if}&\alpha=0\\
0&\text{if}&\alpha<0\end{array}\right..

(4.55)

For the general sequence
(d_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}

we have four cases (if necessary, we can chose an appropriate subsequence):

\lim_{n}d_{n}=\!\left\{\begin{array}[]{clcc}(a)&\infty&&\\
(b)&d>0&\text{with}&\sum_{n}c_{n}^{2}=\infty\\
(c)&d>0&\text{with}&\sum_{n}c_{n}^{2}<\infty\\
(d)&d=0&\end{array}\right.,

(4.56)

where
d_{n}=d(1+c_{n})
and
\lim_{n}c_{n}=0
.

4.4.3
Cases (a), (b), (d)

Remark 4.3
.

In the cases (a)
we see by (
4.46
) that

\qquad\rho_{2}(C_{2},C_{3})=\rho_{3}(C_{2},C_{3})=\infty\quad\text{
for all}\quad(C_{2},C_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}.

The case (d) is reduced to the case (a) by exchanging
(b_{2n},a_{2n})
with
(b_{3n},a_{3n})
.
In the cases (b) by
Lemma
4.36
and (
4.56
) we conclude that

\rho_{2}(C_{2},-C_{3})=\rho_{3}(C_{2},-C_{3})=\infty\quad\text{ for}\quad C_{2}C_{3}^{-1}>0

hence,
\rho_{2}(C_{2},C_{3})=\rho_{3}(C_{2},C_{3})=\infty

for all
(C_{2},C_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}
. Therefore, in cases (a), (b) and (d) we get

x_{2n},\,x_{3n}\,\eta\,\,{\mathfrak{A}}^{3}
.

To finish the proof in these cases, it is sufficient to approximate one of operators
D_{rn},\,\,1\!\leq\!r\!\leq\!3

by opertors
(A_{kn})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
using Lemmas
5.55
–
5.57
, see Section
4.4.4
. Alternatively we can try to approximate
D_{3n},\,D_{2n}
using Lemma
5.64
and its analogue,
see Section
4.4.5
, or to approximate
D_{3n},\,D_{2n}
using Lemma
5.65
and its analogue, see Section
4.4.6
.

Note that by Lemma
4.32
we have
\lim_{n}b_{2n}=\lim_{n}b_{3n}=\infty
.
In the cases (a) and (b) the conditions (
4.39
) are expressed by (
4.56
) as follows:

\displaystyle b=(1,b_{2n},d_{n}b_{2n}),\,\,\,\sum_{n}\frac{1}{b_{2n}^{2}d_{n}}<\infty,\,\,\,\,\sum_{n}\frac{1}{d_{n}}=\infty,\,\,\,\,\lim_{n}d_{n}=\infty,\qquad

(4.57)

\displaystyle b=\big(1,b_{2n},db_{2n}(1+c_{n})\big),\quad\sum_{n}\frac{1}{b_{2n}^{2}}<\infty,\quad\sum_{n}c_{n}^{2}=\infty.\qquad

(4.58)

Indeed, to get (
4.57
) we observe that (
4.39
) are expressed as follows:

\displaystyle S_{1}(3)\!\sim\!\sum_{n}\frac{1}{b_{2n}b_{3n}}=\sum_{n}\frac{1}{b_{2n}^{2}d_{n}}<\infty,\quad S_{2}(3)\sim\sum_{n}\frac{1}{d_{n}}=\infty.

Condition
S_{3}(3)\sim\sum_{n}d_{n}=\infty
holds by
\lim_{n}d_{n}=\infty
.

In order to get (
4.58
), we express the conditions (
4.39
) as follows:

\displaystyle S_{1}(3)\!\sim\!\sum_{n}\frac{1}{b_{2n}db_{2n}(1+c_{n})}\sim\sum_{n}\frac{1}{b_{2n}^{2}}\!<\!\infty,

\displaystyle S_{2}(3)\sim\sum_{n}\frac{1}{d_{n}}=\sum_{n}\frac{1}{1+c_{n}}=\infty,\quad S_{3}(3)\sim\sum_{n}d_{n}\!=\!\sum_{n}(1+c_{n})\!=\!\infty.

The conditions
S_{2}(3)=\infty
holds by
\lim_{n}c_{n}=0
.

4.4.4
Approximation of
D_{rn},\,\,1\leq r\leq 3
, 1

By Lemmas

–
5.57
we have for

(see (

)):

D_{kn}\,\,\eta\,\,{\mathfrak{A}}^{3}\Leftrightarrow\Delta_{k}=\infty,\quad\text{where}\quad\Delta_{k}:=\Delta(Y_{k},Y_{r},Y_{s}),

and

.

Recall that by (

) we have

\|Y_{1}\|^{2}\sim\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}b_{1n}a_{1n}^{2},\quad\|Y_{2}\|^{2}\sim\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}b_{1n}a_{2n}^{2},\quad\|Y_{3}\|^{2}\sim\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}b_{1n}a_{3n}^{2}.

(4.59)

Since
\|Y_{1}\|^{2}\sim\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}b_{1n}a^{2}_{1n}\sim S^{L}_{11}(\mu)=\infty
, we have four
possibilities for
y_{23}:=(y_{2},y_{3})\in\{0,1\}^{2}
:

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}&(1.0)&(1.1)&(1.2)&(1.3)\\
y_{1}&1&1&1&1\\
y_{2}&0&1&0&1\\
y_{3}&0&0&1&1\\
\end{array}

(4.60)

In the case (1.0) we have
\Delta(Y_{1},Y_{2},Y_{3})\sim\|Y_{1}\|^{2}=\infty
, so we can approximate
