Lemma 5.66
.

We have

\displaystyle D_{3k}{\bf 1}\in\langle\sin\big(s_{k}(x_{3k}-a_{3k})\big)A_{kn}{\bf 1}\mid k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\rangle\,\,\Leftrightarrow\,\,\Sigma_{3}(D,s)=\infty,

(5.38)

\displaystyle D_{3k}{\bf 1}\in\langle\cos\big(s_{k}(x_{3k}-a_{3k})\big)A_{kn}{\bf 1}\mid k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\rangle\,\,\Leftrightarrow\,\,\Sigma^{\vee}_{3}(D,s)=\infty,

(5.39)

\displaystyle\text{where}\,\,\Sigma_{3}(D,s)\!=\!\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{|M\eta_{3k}(s_{k})|^{2}}{\|g_{k}(s_{k})\|^{2}},\,\,\Sigma^{\vee}_{3}(D,s)\!=\!\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{|M\eta^{\vee}_{3k}(s_{k})|^{2}}{\|g^{\vee}_{k}(s_{k})\|^{2}},\qquad

(5.40)

\displaystyle\text{moreover,}\quad\Sigma_{3}(D,s^{(3)})\sim\Sigma_{3}(D):=\sum_{k}\frac{\frac{1}{2b_{3k}}}{C_{k}+a^{2}_{1k}+a^{2}_{2k}+a^{2}_{3k}},

(5.41)

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad\Sigma^{\vee}_{3}(D,s^{(3)})\sim\Sigma^{\vee}_{3}(D):=\sum_{k}\frac{a^{2}_{3k}}{C_{k}+a^{2}_{1k}+a^{2}_{2k}+a^{2}_{3k}},

(5.42)

where
s^{(3)}=(s_{3k})_{k}

with
\frac{s^{2}_{3k}}{b_{3k}}\equiv 1,\,\,k\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof 36
.

We shall try
to obtain separately the real part and imaginary part of
M\xi_{3k}(s)
, where

\xi_{3k}(s_{k})=ix_{3k}\exp\big(is_{k}(x_{3k}-a_{3k})\big)
.
Using Lemma
5.65

formulas (
5.28
) and (
5.33
) we get

\displaystyle H_{a,b}(s)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}ix\exp\big(is(x-a)\big)d\mu_{(b,a)}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}i(x+a)\exp(isx)d\mu_{(b,0)}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{dF_{b}(s)}{ds}+iaF_{b}(s)=\Big(-\frac{s}{2b}+ia\Big)\exp\Big(-\frac{s^{2}}{4b}\Big)=:M\xi_{3k}(s),

Recall the Euler formulas

\displaystyle e^{it}=\cos t+i\sin t,\quad e^{-it}=\cos t-i\sin t,

(5.43)

\displaystyle\cos t=\frac{e^{it}+e^{-it}}{2},\quad\sin t=\frac{e^{it}-e^{-it}}{2i}.

More precisely, we denote for
1\leq r\leq 3

\eta_{rk}(s)=x_{rk}\cos\big(s_{k}(x_{3k}-a_{3k})\big),\quad\eta^{\vee}_{rk}(s)=x_{rk}\cos\big(s_{k}(x_{3k}-a_{3k})\big).

(5.44)

We determine when the inclusion holds:

\displaystyle D_{3k}{\bf 1}\in\langle\sin\big(s_{k}(x_{3k}-a_{3k})\big)A_{kn}{\bf 1}=\Big(x_{1k}\sin\big(s_{k}(x_{3k}-a_{3k})\big)D_{1n}

\displaystyle+x_{2k}\sin\big(s_{k}(x_{3k}-a_{3k})\big)D_{2n}+x_{3k}\sin\big(s_{k}(x_{3k}-a_{3k})\big)D_{3n}\Big){\bf 1}\mid k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\rangle,

\displaystyle D_{3k}{\bf 1}\in\langle\cos\big(s_{k}(x_{3k}-a_{3k})\big)A_{kn}{\bf 1}=\Big(x_{1k}\cos\big(s_{k}(x_{3k}-a_{3k})\big)D_{1n}

\displaystyle+x_{2k}\cos\big(s_{k}(x_{3k}-a_{3k})\big)D_{2n}+x_{3k}\cos\big(s_{k}(x_{3k}-a_{3k})\big)D_{3n}\Big){\bf 1}\mid k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\rangle.

Set

\displaystyle g_{k}(s_{k})\!=\!\Big(\eta_{1k}(s_{k})D_{1n}+\eta_{2k}(s_{k})D_{2n}\!+\!\big[\eta_{3k}(s_{k})\!-\!M\eta_{3k}(s_{k})\big]D_{3n}\Big){\bf 1},\qquad

(5.45)

\displaystyle g^{\vee}_{k}(s_{k})\!=\!\Big(\eta^{\vee}_{1k}(s_{k})D_{1n}\!+\!\eta^{\vee}_{2k}(s_{k})D_{2n}\!+\!\big[\eta^{\vee}_{3k}(s_{k})\!-\!M\eta^{\vee}_{3k}(s_{k})\big]D_{3n}\Big){\bf 1},

(5.46)

We show that (compare with (
5.28
))

\displaystyle M\eta_{3k}(s)=-\frac{1}{2}\Big(H_{a,b}(s)+\overline{H_{a,b}(s)}\Big)=\frac{s}{2b_{3k}}\exp\Big(-\frac{s^{2}}{4b_{3k}}\Big),

(5.47)

\displaystyle M\eta^{\vee}_{3k}(s)=\frac{1}{2i}\Big(H_{a,b}(s)-\overline{H_{a,b}(s)}\Big)=a_{3k}\exp\Big(-\frac{s^{2}}{4b_{3k}}\Big).

(5.48)

Recall the definition of the function
F_{b}(s)

defined by (
5.31
):

F_{b}(s)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\exp\big(is(x-a)\big)d\mu_{(b,a)}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\exp\big(isx\big)d\mu_{(b,0)}(x)=\exp\Big(-\frac{s^{2}}{4b}\Big).

(5.49)

We have

\displaystyle M\eta(s)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}x\sin\big(s(x-a)\big)d\mu_{(b,a)}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}(x+a)\sin(sx)d\mu_{(b,0)}(x)=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}(x+a)\frac{e^{isx}-e^{-isx}}{2i}d\mu_{(b,0)}(x)=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}i(x+a)\big(e^{isx}-e^{-isx}\big)d\mu_{(b,0)}(x)=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\Big(H_{a,b}(s)+\overline{H_{a,b}(s)}\Big)=\frac{s}{2b}\exp\Big(-\frac{s^{2}}{4b}\Big),

this implies (
5.47
).
Similarly we get

\displaystyle M\eta^{\vee}(s)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}x\cos\big(s(x-a)\big)d\mu_{(b,a)}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}(x+a)\cos(sx)d\mu_{(b,0)}(x)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2i}\int_{\mathbb{R}}i(x+a)\Big(e^{isx}+e^{-isx}\Big)d\mu_{(b,0)}(x)=\frac{1}{2i}\Big(H_{a,b}(s)-\overline{H_{a,b}(s)}\Big)

\displaystyle=a\exp\Big(-\frac{s^{2}}{4b}\Big)

this implies (
5.47
).
Fix
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
we put
\sum_{k=-m}^{m}t_{k}M\eta_{3k}(s_{k})=(t,b)=1
,
where
t=(t_{k})_{k=-m}^{m}
and
b=(M\eta_{3k}(s_{k}))_{k=-m}^{m}
. We have

\displaystyle\|\big[\sum_{k=-m}^{m}t_{k}\sin\big(s_{k}(x_{3k}-a_{3k})\big)A_{kn}-D_{3n}\big]{\bf 1}\|^{2}

(5.50)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|\sum_{k=-m}^{m}t_{k}\Big(\eta_{1k}(s_{k})D_{1n}+\eta_{2k}(s_{k})D_{2n}+\big[\eta_{3k}(s_{k})-M\eta_{3k}(s_{k})\big]D_{3n}\Big){\bf 1}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=-m}^{m}t^{2}_{k}\|g_{k}(s_{k})\|^{2},\,\,\,\text{since}\,\,\,\Big(D_{rn}{\bf 1},D_{ln}{\bf 1}\Big)\!=\!0,\,\,\,1\leq r<l\leq 3,

where the
g_{k}(s_{k})
are defined by (
5.45
).
To calculate
\|g_{k}(s_{k})\|^{2}
we have

\displaystyle\|g_{k}(s_{k})\|^{2}=(g_{k}(s_{k}),g_{k}(s_{k}))=

\displaystyle\Big(\big(\eta_{1k}(s_{k})D_{1n}+\eta_{2k}(s_{k})D_{2n}+\big[\eta_{3k}(s_{k})-M\eta_{3k}(s_{k})\big]D_{3n}\big){\bf 1},

\displaystyle\big(\eta_{1k}(s_{k})D_{1n}+\eta_{2k}(s_{k})D_{2n}+\big[\eta_{3k}(s_{k})-M\eta_{3k}(s_{k})\big]D_{3n}\big){\bf 1}\Big)=

\displaystyle\|x_{1k}{\bf 1}\|^{2}\|\sin\big(s_{k}(x_{3k}-a_{3k})\big){\bf 1}\|^{2}\|D_{1k}{\bf 1}\|^{2}+

\displaystyle\|x_{2k}{\bf 1}\|^{2}\|\sin\big(s_{k}(x_{3k}-a_{3k})\big){\bf 1}\|^{2}\|D_{2k}{\bf 1}\|^{2}+

\displaystyle\Big(M|\eta_{kn}(s_{k})|^{2}-|M\eta_{kn}(s_{k})|^{2}\Big)\|D_{3k}{\bf 1}\|^{2}=\Big(\frac{1}{2b_{1k}}+a^{2}_{1k}\Big)I_{3}\frac{b_{1n}}{2}+

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{1}{2b_{2k}}+a^{2}_{2k}\Big)I_{3}\frac{b_{2n}}{2}+\Big(M|\eta_{kn}(s_{k})|^{2}-|M\eta_{kn}(s_{k})|^{2}\Big)\frac{b_{3n}}{2}.

(5.51)

We need to calculate
I_{3}=\|\sin\big(s_{k}(x_{3k}-a_{3k})\big){\bf 1}\|^{2},\,\,M|\eta_{kn}(s_{k})|^{2}
and
|M\eta_{kn}(s_{k})|^{2}
.
If we set
a:=a_{3k},\,\,b:=b_{3k}
, we get

\displaystyle I_{3}=\|\sin\big(s_{k}(x_{3k}-a_{3k})\big){\bf 1}\|^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{e^{isx}-e^{-isx}}{2i}\frac{e^{-isx}-e^{isx}}{-2i}d\mu_{(b,0)}(x)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Big(1-\frac{e^{2isx}+e^{-2isx}}{2}\Big)d\mu_{(b,0)}(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{F_b(a).1}}}{{=}}\frac{1-e^{-\frac{s^{2}}{b}}}{2},

(5.52)

\displaystyle|M\eta_{kn}(s_{k})|^{2}=\frac{s^{2}_{k}}{4b^{2}_{3k}}\exp\Big(-\frac{s_{k}^{2}}{2b_{3k}}\Big),

(5.53)

\displaystyle M|\eta_{kn}(s_{k})|^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}(x^{2}+2xa+a^{2})\frac{e^{isx}-e^{-isx}}{2i}\frac{e^{-isx}-e^{isx}}{-2i}d\mu_{(b,0)}(x)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(x^{2}+2xa+a^{2})\Big(1-\frac{e^{2isx}+e^{-2isx}}{2}\Big)d\mu_{(b,0)}(x)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\Big[\int_{\mathbb{R}}(x^{2}+a^{2})d\mu_{(b,0)}(x)-\int_{\mathbb{R}}(x^{2}+a^{2})\frac{e^{2isx}+e^{-2isx}}{2}d\mu_{(b,0)}(x)\Big]=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\Big[\frac{1}{2b}+a^{2}-\frac{d^{2}F_{b}(2s)}{ds^{2}}-a^{2}F_{b}(2s)\Big]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{F_b(a).1}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{2}\Big[\frac{1}{2b}+a^{2}-\frac{1}{(2i)^{2}}\Big[\Big(\frac{2s}{b}\Big)^{2}-\frac{2}{b}\Big]\times

\displaystyle e^{-\frac{s^{2}}{b}}-a^{2}e^{-\frac{s^{2}}{b}}\Big]=\frac{1}{2}\Big[\Big(\frac{1}{2b}+a^{2}\Big)(1-e^{-\frac{s^{2}}{b}})+\frac{s^{2}}{b^{2}}e^{-\frac{s^{2}}{b}}\Big].

(5.54)

Finally, we get

M|\eta_{kn}(s_{k})|^{2}-|M\eta_{kn}(s_{k})|^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\Big[\Big(\frac{1}{2b}+a^{2}\Big)(1-e^{-\frac{s^{2}}{b}})+\frac{s^{2}}{b^{2}}e^{-\frac{s^{2}}{b}}\Big]-\frac{s^{2}}{4b^{2}}e^{-\frac{s^{2}}{2b}}.

(5.55)

By (
5.60
),(
5.51
), (
5.52
), (
5.55
) and (
6.3
) we prove (
5.38
), where

\displaystyle\Sigma_{3}(D,s)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{|M\eta_{kn}(s_{k})|^{2}}{\|g_{k}(s_{k})\|^{2}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{\frac{s^{2}_{k}}{4b^{2}_{3k}}e^{-\frac{s_{k}^{2}}{2b_{3k}}}}{\Big(\frac{1}{2b_{1k}}+a^{2}_{1k}\Big)I_{3}\frac{b_{1n}}{2}+\Big(\frac{1}{2b_{2k}}+a^{2}_{2k}\Big)I_{3}\frac{b_{2n}}{2}+\Big(M|\eta_{kn}(s_{k})|^{2}-|M\eta_{kn}(s_{k})|^{2}\Big)\frac{b_{3n}}{2}}\sim

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{\frac{s^{2}_{k}}{4b^{2}_{3k}}e^{-\frac{s_{k}^{2}}{2b_{3k}}}}{\frac{1-e^{-\frac{s^{2}_{k}}{b_{3k}}}}{2}\Big(c_{1k}+c_{2k}\Big)+\frac{1}{2}\Big[c_{3k}(1-e^{-\frac{s^{2}_{k}}{b_{3k}}})+\frac{s^{2}_{k}}{b_{3k}^{2}}e^{-\frac{s^{2}_{k}}{b_{3k}}}\Big]-\frac{s^{2}_{k}}{4b_{3k}^{2}}e^{-\frac{s^{2}_{k}}{2b_{3k}}}}=

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{\frac{x^{2}_{k}}{4b_{3k}}e^{-\frac{x_{k}^{2}}{2}}}{\frac{1-e^{-x^{2}_{k}}}{2}\Big(c_{1k}+c_{2k}\Big)\!+\!\frac{1}{2}\Big[c_{3k}(1\!-\!e^{-x^{2}_{k}})+\frac{x^{2}_{k}}{b_{3k}}e^{-x_{k}^{2}}\Big]\!-\!\frac{x_{k}^{2}}{4b_{3k}}e^{-\frac{x^{2}_{k}}{2}}}=:\Sigma_{3}(D,x),

(5.56)

where
x^{2}_{k}=\frac{s^{2}_{k}}{b_{3k}}
and
c_{rk}=\frac{1}{2b_{rk}}+a^{2}_{rk}
. For
x^{(3)}=(x_{k})_{k}

with
x_{k}\equiv 1
we get

\displaystyle\Sigma_{3}(D,x^{(3)})=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{\frac{1}{4b_{3k}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}}}{\frac{1-e^{-1}}{2}\Big(c_{1k}+c_{2k}\Big)+\frac{1}{2}\Big[c_{3k}(1-e^{-1})+\frac{1}{b_{3k}}e^{-1}\Big]-\frac{1}{4b_{3k}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{\frac{1}{4b_{3k}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}}}{\frac{1-e^{-1}}{2}\Big(c_{1k}+c_{2k}+c_{3k}\Big)+\frac{1}{2b_{3k}}\Big(e^{-1}-\frac{1}{2}e^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Big)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{r.two-sim-ser}}}{{\sim}}

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{\frac{1}{2b_{3k}}}{c_{1k}+c_{2k}+c_{3k}}\!=\!\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{\frac{1}{2b_{3k}}}{\frac{1}{2b_{1k}}+\frac{1}{2b_{2k}}+\frac{1}{2b_{3k}}+a^{2}_{1k}+a^{2}_{2k}+a^{2}_{3k}}=\Sigma_{3}(D).

(5.57)

So, we have proved (
5.41
)
for
x=(x_{k})_{k}

with
x_{k}\equiv 1
.
To approximate
D_{3n}

in terms of functions involving the cosine,
fix
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
and put
\sum_{k=-m}^{m}t_{k}M\eta^{\vee}_{3k}(s_{k})=(t,b)=1
,
where
t=(t_{k})_{k=-m}^{m}
and
b=(M\eta^{\vee}_{3k}(s_{k}))_{k=-m}^{m}
. We have

\displaystyle\|\big[\sum_{k=-m}^{m}t_{k}\cos\big(s_{k}(x_{3k}-a_{3k})\big)A_{kn}-D_{3n}\big]{\bf 1}\|^{2}

(5.58)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|\sum_{k=-m}^{m}t_{k}\Big(\eta^{\vee}_{1k}(s_{k})D_{1n}+\eta^{\vee}_{2k}(s_{k})D_{2n}+\big[\eta^{\vee}_{3k}(s_{k})-M\eta^{\vee}_{3k}(s_{k})\big]D_{3n}\Big){\bf 1}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=-m}^{m}t^{2}_{k}\|g^{\vee}_{k}(s_{k})\|^{2},\,\,\,\text{since}\,\,\,\Big(D_{rn}{\bf 1},D_{ln}{\bf 1}\Big)\!=\!0,\,\,\,1\leq r<l\leq 3,

where the
g^{\vee}_{k}(s_{k})
are defined by (
5.45
).
To calculate
\|g^{\vee}_{k}(s_{k})\|^{2}
we have

\displaystyle\|g^{\vee}_{k}(s_{k})\|^{2}=(g^{\vee}_{k}(s_{k}),g^{\vee}_{k}(s_{k}))=

\displaystyle\Big(\big(\eta^{\vee}_{1k}(s_{k})D_{1n}+\eta^{\vee}_{2k}(s_{k})D_{2n}+\big[\eta^{\vee}_{3k}(s_{k})-M\eta^{\vee}_{3k}(s_{k})\big]D_{3n}\big){\bf 1},

\displaystyle\big(\eta^{\vee}_{1k}(s_{k})D_{1n}+\eta^{\vee}_{2k}(s_{k})D_{2n}+\big[\eta^{\vee}_{3k}(s_{k})-M\eta^{\vee}_{3k}(s_{k})\big]D_{3n}\big){\bf 1}\Big)=

\displaystyle\|x_{1k}{\bf 1}\|^{2}\|\cos\big(s_{k}(x_{3k}-a_{3k})\big){\bf 1}\|^{2}\|D_{1k}{\bf 1}\|^{2}+

\displaystyle\|x_{2k}{\bf 1}\|^{2}\|\cos\big(s_{k}(x_{3k}-a_{3k})\big){\bf 1}\|^{2}\|D_{2k}{\bf 1}\|^{2}+

\displaystyle\Big(M|\eta^{\vee}_{kn}(s_{k})|^{2}-|M\eta^{\vee}_{kn}(s_{k})|^{2}\Big)\|D_{3k}{\bf 1}\|^{2}=\Big(\frac{1}{2b_{1k}}+a^{2}_{1k}\Big)I^{\vee}_{3}\frac{b_{1n}}{2}+

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{1}{2b_{2k}}+a^{2}_{2k}\Big)I^{\vee}_{3}\frac{b_{2n}}{2}+\Big(M|\eta^{\vee}_{kn}(s_{k})|^{2}-|M\eta^{\vee}_{kn}(s_{k})|^{2}\Big)\frac{b_{3n}}{2}.

(5.59)

We need to calculate
I^{\vee}_{3}=\|\cos\big(is_{k}(x_{3k}-a_{3k})\big){\bf 1}\|^{2},\,\,M|\eta^{\vee}_{kn}(s_{k})|^{2}
and
|M\eta^{\vee}_{kn}(s_{k})|^{2}
.
Finally, we get (we set
b:=b_{3k}
)
To approximate
D_{3n}

in terms of functions involving
\cos
,
fix
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
and put
\sum_{k=-m}^{m}t_{k}M\eta^{\vee}_{3k}(s_{k})=(t,b)=1
,
where
t=(t_{k})_{k=-m}^{m}
and
b=(M\eta^{\vee}_{3k}(s_{k}))_{k=-m}^{m}
. We have

\displaystyle\|\big[\sum_{k=-m}^{m}t_{k}\cos\big(s_{k}(x_{3k}-a_{3k})\big)A_{kn}-D_{3n}\big]{\bf 1}\|^{2}

(5.60)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|\sum_{k=-m}^{m}t_{k}\Big(\eta^{\vee}_{1k}(s_{k})D_{1n}+\eta^{\vee}_{2k}(s_{k})D_{2n}+\big[\eta^{\vee}_{3k}(s_{k})-M\eta^{\vee}_{3k}(s_{k})\big]D_{3n}\Big){\bf 1}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=-m}^{m}t^{2}_{k}\|g^{\vee}_{k}(s_{k})\|^{2},\,\,\,\text{since}\,\,\,\Big(D_{rn}{\bf 1},D_{ln}{\bf 1}\Big)\!=\!0,\,\,\,1\leq r<l\leq 3,

where the
g^{\vee}_{k}(s_{k})
are defined by (
5.45
).
To calculate
\|g^{\vee}_{k}(s_{k})\|^{2}
we have

\displaystyle\|g^{\vee}_{k}(s_{k})\|^{2}=(g^{\vee}_{k}(s_{k}),g^{\vee}_{k}(s_{k}))=

\displaystyle\Big(\big(\eta^{\vee}_{1k}(s_{k})D_{1n}+\eta^{\vee}_{2k}(s_{k})D_{2n}+\big[\eta^{\vee}_{3k}(s_{k})-M\eta^{\vee}_{3k}(s_{k})\big]D_{3n}\big){\bf 1},

\displaystyle\big(\eta^{\vee}_{1k}(s_{k})D_{1n}+\eta^{\vee}_{2k}(s_{k})D_{2n}+\big[\eta^{\vee}_{3k}(s_{k})-M\eta^{\vee}_{3k}(s_{k})\big]D_{3n}\big){\bf 1}\Big)=

\displaystyle\|x_{1k}{\bf 1}\|^{2}\|\cos\big(s_{k}(x_{3k}-a_{3k})\big){\bf 1}\|^{2}\|D_{1k}{\bf 1}\|^{2}+

\displaystyle\|x_{2k}{\bf 1}\|^{2}\|\cos\big(s_{k}(x_{3k}-a_{3k})\big){\bf 1}\|^{2}\|D_{2k}{\bf 1}\|^{2}+

\displaystyle\lx@nobreakspace\Big(M|\eta^{\vee}_{kn}(s_{k})|^{2}-|M\eta^{\vee}_{kn}(s_{k})|^{2}\Big)\|D_{3k}{\bf 1}\|^{2}=\Big(\frac{1}{2b_{1k}}+a^{2}_{1k}\Big)I^{\vee}_{3}\frac{b_{1n}}{2}+

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{1}{2b_{2k}}+a^{2}_{2k}\Big)I^{\vee}_{3}\frac{b_{2n}}{2}+\lx@nobreakspace\Big(M|\eta^{\vee}_{kn}(s_{k})|^{2}-|M\eta^{\vee}_{kn}(s_{k})|^{2}\Big)\frac{b_{3n}}{2}.

(5.61)

We need to calculate
I^{\vee}_{3}=\|\cos\big(s_{k}(x_{3k}-a_{3k})\big){\bf 1}\|^{2},\,\,M|\eta^{\vee}_{kn}(s_{k})|^{2}
and
|M\eta^{\vee}_{kn}(s_{k})|^{2}
.
If we set
b:=b_{3k}
, we get

\displaystyle I^{\vee}_{3}=\|\cos\big(s_{k}(x_{3k}-a_{3k})\big){\bf 1}\|^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{e^{isx}+e^{-isx}}{2}\frac{e^{-isx}+e^{isx}}{2}d\mu_{(b,0)}(x)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Big(1+\frac{e^{2isx}+e^{-2isx}}{2}\Big)d\mu_{(b,0)}(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{F_b(a).1}}}{{=}}\frac{1+e^{-\frac{s^{2}}{b}}}{2},

(5.62)

\displaystyle|M\eta^{\vee}_{kn}(s_{k})|^{2}=a^{2}_{3k}\exp\Big(-\frac{s_{k}^{2}}{2b_{3k}}\Big),

(5.63)

\displaystyle M|\eta^{\vee}_{kn}(s_{k})|^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}(x^{2}+2xa+a^{2})\frac{e^{isx}+e^{-isx}}{2}\frac{e^{-isx}+e^{isx}}{2}d\mu_{(b,0)}(x)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(x^{2}+2xa+a^{2})\Big(1+\frac{e^{2isx}+e^{-2isx}}{2}\Big)d\mu_{(b,0)}(x)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\Big[\int_{\mathbb{R}}(x^{2}+a^{2})d\mu_{(b,0)}(x)+\int_{\mathbb{R}}(x^{2}+a^{2})\frac{e^{2isx}+e^{-2isx}}{2}d\mu_{(b,0)}(x)\Big]=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\Big[\frac{1}{2b}+a^{2}+\frac{d^{2}F_{b}(2s)}{ds^{2}}+a^{2}F_{b}(2s)\Big]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{F_b(a).1}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{2}\Big[\frac{1}{2b}+a^{2}+\frac{1}{(2i)^{2}}\Big[\Big(\frac{2s}{b}\Big)^{2}-\frac{2}{b}\Big]\times

\displaystyle e^{-\frac{s^{2}}{b}}+a^{2}e^{-\frac{s^{2}}{b}}\Big]=\frac{1}{2}\Big[\Big(\frac{1}{2b}+a^{2}\Big)(1+e^{-\frac{s^{2}}{b}})-\frac{s^{2}}{b^{2}}e^{-\frac{s^{2}}{b}}\Big].

(5.64)

Finally, we get

M|\eta^{\vee}_{kn}(s_{k})|^{2}\!-|M\eta^{\vee}_{kn}(s_{k})|^{2}\!=\!\frac{1}{2}\Big[\Big(\frac{1}{2b}+a^{2}\Big)(1+e^{-\frac{s^{2}}{b}})-\frac{s^{2}}{b^{2}}e^{-\frac{s^{2}}{b}}\Big]-{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}a^{2}}e^{-\frac{s^{2}}{2b}}.

(5.65)

By (
5.58
), (
5.59
), (
5.62
), (
5.65
) and (
6.3
) we prove (
5.39
), where

\displaystyle\Sigma^{\vee}_{3}(D,s)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{|M\eta^{\vee}_{kn}(s_{k})|^{2}}{\|g^{\vee}_{k}(s_{k})\|^{2}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{a^{2}_{3k}e^{-\frac{s_{k}^{2}}{2b_{3k}}}}{\Big(\frac{1}{2b_{1k}}+a^{2}_{1k}\Big)I^{\vee}_{3}\frac{b_{1n}}{2}+\Big(\frac{1}{2b_{2k}}+a^{2}_{2k}\Big)I^{\vee}_{3}\frac{b_{2n}}{2}+\Big(M|\eta^{\vee}_{kn}(s_{k})|^{2}-|M\eta^{\vee}_{kn}(s_{k})|^{2}\Big)\frac{b_{3n}}{2}}

\displaystyle\sim\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{a^{2}_{3k}e^{-\frac{s_{k}^{2}}{2b_{3k}}}}{\frac{1+e^{-\frac{s^{2}_{k}}{b_{3k}}}}{2}\Big(c_{1k}+c_{2k}\Big)+\frac{1}{2}\Big[c_{3k}(1+e^{-\frac{s^{2}_{k}}{b_{3k}}})-\frac{s^{2}_{k}}{b_{3k}^{2}}e^{-\frac{s^{2}_{k}}{b_{3k}}}\Big]-a^{2}_{3k}e^{-\frac{s^{2}_{k}}{2b_{3k}}}}=

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{a^{2}_{3k}e^{-\frac{x_{k}^{2}}{2}}}{\frac{1-e^{-x^{2}_{k}}}{2}\Big(c_{1k}\!+\!c_{2k}\Big)\!+\!\frac{1}{2}\Big[c_{3k}(1+e^{-x^{2}_{k}})-\frac{x^{2}_{k}}{b_{3k}}e^{-x_{k}^{2}}\Big]\!-\!a^{2}_{3k}e^{-\frac{x^{2}_{k}}{2}}}\!=\!\Sigma_{3}(D,x),\quad

(5.66)

where
x^{2}_{k}=\frac{s^{2}_{k}}{b_{3k}}
and
c_{rk}=\frac{1}{2b_{rk}}+a^{2}_{rk}
. For
x^{(3)}=(x_{k})_{k}

with
x_{k}\equiv 1
we get

\displaystyle\Sigma^{\vee}_{3}(D,x^{(3)})=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{a^{2}_{3k}e^{-\frac{1}{2}}}{\frac{1+e^{-1}}{2}\Big(c_{1k}+c_{2k}\Big)+\frac{1}{2}\Big[c_{3k}(1+e^{-1})-\frac{1}{b_{3k}}e^{-1}\Big]-a^{2}_{3k}e^{-\frac{1}{2}}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{a^{2}_{3k}e^{-\frac{1}{2}}}{\frac{1+e^{-1}}{2}\Big(c_{1k}+c_{2k}+c_{3k}\Big)-\Big(\frac{1}{2b_{3k}}e^{-1}+a^{2}_{3k}e^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Big)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{r.two-sim-ser}}}{{\sim}}

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{a^{2}_{3k}}{c_{1k}+c_{2k}+c_{3k}}\!=\!\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{a^{2}_{3k}}{C_{k}+a^{2}_{1k}+a^{2}_{2k}+a^{2}_{3k}}=\Sigma^{\vee}_{3}(D).

(5.67)

So, we have proved (
5.42
)
for
x=(x_{k})_{k}

with
x_{k}\equiv 1
.
∎

6
How far is a vector from a hyperplane?

6.1
Some estimates

We recall some material from
[
27
]
, Section 1.4.1, pp. 24–25.

Lemma 6.67

(
[
24
]
)
.

For a strictly positive operator
A

(
i.e.,
(Af,f)>0
for
f\not=0
)
acting in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and a vector

b\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\backslash\{0\}
, we have

\min_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\Big((Ax,x)\mid(x,b)=1\Big)=\frac{1}{(A^{-1}b,b)}.

(6.1)

The minimum is assumed for

x=\frac{A^{-1}b}{(A^{-1}b,b)}.

Lemma
6.67
is a direct generalization of the well known
result (see, for example,
[
4
]
, Chap. I, §52),
stating that
for
a_{k}>0,\,\,1\leq k\leq n
we have

\min_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{n}a_{k}x_{k}^{2}\mid\sum_{k=1}^{n}x_{k}=1\Big)=\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{1}{a_{k}}\Big)^{-1}.

(6.2)

We will also use the same result in a slightly different
form:

\min_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{n}a_{k}x_{k}^{2}\mid\sum_{k=1}^{n}x_{k}b_{k}=1\Big)=\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{b_{k}^{2}}{a_{k}}\Big)^{-1},

(6.3)

with the minimum being assumed
for

x_{k}=\frac{b_{k}}{a_{k}}\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{b_{k}^{2}}{a_{k}}\Big)^{-1}
.

6.2
The distance of a vector from a hyperplane

We follow closely the exposition
[
30
]
. We start with a classical result, see, e.g.

[
9
]
.
Consider the hyperplane
V_{n}
generated by
n
arbitrary vectors
f_{1},\dots,f_{n}
in some Hilbert space
H
.

Lemma 6.68
.

The square of the distance
d(f_{0},V_{n})
of a vector
f_{0}
from the hyperplane
V_{n}
is given by the ratio of two Gram determinants:

d^{2}(f_{0},V_{n})=\frac{\Gamma(f_{0},f_{1},f_{2},\dots,f_{n})}{\Gamma(f_{1},f_{2},\dots,f_{n})}.

(6.4)

6.3
Gram determinants and Gram matrices

Definition 6.1
.

Let us recall the definition of
the Gram determinant and the Gram matrix (see

[
9
]
, Chap IX, §5). Given the vectors
x_{1},x_{2},...,x_{m}
in some
Hilbert space
H
the Gram
matrix
\gamma(x_{1},x_{2},...,x_{m})
is defined by the formula

\gamma(x_{1},x_{2},...,x_{m})=\big((x_{k},x_{n})\big)_{k,n=1}^{m}.

The determinant of this matrix is called the Gram determinant for
the vectors
x_{1},x_{2},...,x_{m}
and is denoted by

\Gamma(x_{1},x_{2},...,x_{m})
.
Thus,

\Gamma(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{m}):={\rm det}\,\gamma(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{m}).

(6.5)

6.4
The generalized characteristic polynomial
and its properties

Notations
. For a matrix
C\in{\rm Mat}(n,\mathbb{R})
and
1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}<\dots i_{r}\leq n,

1\leq j_{1}<j_{2}<\dots j_{r}\leq n,\,\,r\leq n

denote by

M^{i_{1}i_{2}\dots i_{r}}_{j_{1}j_{2}\dots j_{r}}(C)\quad\text{ and}\quad A^{i_{1}i_{2}\dots i_{r}}_{j_{1}j_{2}\dots j_{r}}(C)

the corresponding
minors
and
cofactors
of the matrix
C
.

Definition 6.2
.

(
[
27
, Ch.1.4.3]
)

For the matrix
C\in{\rm Mat}(m,{\mathbb{C}})
and
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{m})\in{\mathbb{C}}^{m}
define the generalization of the characteristic polynomial,
p_{C}(t)={\rm det}\,(tI-C),\,t\in{\mathbb{C}}

as follows:

P_{C}(\lambda)={\rm det}\,C(\lambda),\quad\text{where}\quad C(\lambda)={\rm diag}(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{m})+C.

(6.6)

Lemma 6.69
.

(
[
27
, Ch.1.4.3]
)

For the generalized characteristic polynomial
P_{C}(\lambda)

of

C\!\in\!{\rm Mat}(m,{\mathbb{C}})
and

\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},...,\lambda_{m})\in{\mathbb{C}}^{m}
we have

P_{C}(\lambda)={\rm det}\,C+\sum_{r=1}^{m}\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}<...<i_{r}\leq m}\lambda_{i_{1}}\lambda_{i_{2}}...\lambda_{i_{r}}A^{i_{1}i_{2}...i_{r}}_{i_{1}i_{2}...i_{r}}(C).

(6.7)

Remark 6.1
.

If we set

\lambda_{\alpha}=\lambda_{i_{1}}\lambda_{i_{2}}...\lambda_{i_{r}}
, where

\alpha=(i_{1},i_{2},...,i_{r})
and

A^{\alpha}_{\alpha}(C)=A^{i_{1}i_{2}...i_{r}}_{i_{1}i_{2}...i_{r}}(C),\,\,M^{\alpha}_{\alpha}(C)=M^{i_{1}i_{2}...i_{r}}_{i_{1}i_{2}...i_{r}}(C),\,\,\lambda_{\emptyset}=1,\,\,A^{\emptyset}_{\emptyset}(C)={\rm det}\,C
we
may write (
6.7
) as follows:

P_{C}(\lambda)={\rm det}\,C(\lambda)=\sum_{\emptyset\subseteq\alpha\subseteq\{1,2,...,m\}}\lambda_{\alpha}A^{\alpha}_{\alpha}(C),

(6.8)

P_{C}(\lambda)={\rm det}\,C(\lambda)=\left(\prod_{k=1}^{n}\lambda_{k}\right)\sum_{\emptyset\subseteq\alpha\subseteq\{1,2,...,m\}}\frac{M^{\alpha}_{\alpha}(C)}{\lambda_{\alpha}},

(6.9)

Let

X=X_{mn}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}x_{11}&x_{12}&...&x_{1n}\\
x_{21}&x_{22}&...&x_{2n}\\
...&...&...&...\\
x_{m1}&x_{m2}&...&x_{mn}\end{array}\right).

(6.10)

Setting

x_{k}=(x_{1k},x_{2k},...,x_{mk})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m},\quad y_{r}=(x_{r1},x_{r2},...,x_{rn})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n},

(6.11)

we get

X^{*}X=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}(x_{1},x_{1})&(x_{1},x_{2})&...&(x_{1},x_{n})\\
(x_{2},x_{1})&(x_{2},x_{2})&...&(x_{2},x_{n})\\
...&...&...&...\\
(x_{n},x_{1})&(x_{n},x_{2})&...&(x_{n},x_{n})\end{array}\right)=\gamma(x_{1},x_{2},...,x_{n}),

(6.12)

XX^{*}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}(y_{1},y_{1})&(y_{1},y_{2})&...&(y_{1},y_{m})\\
(y_{2},y_{1})&(y_{2},y_{2})&...&(y_{2},y_{m})\\
...&...&...&...\\
(y_{m},y_{1})&(y_{m},y_{2})&...&(y_{m},y_{m})\end{array}\right)=\gamma(y_{1},y_{2},...,y_{m}),

(6.13)

therefore, we obtain

\ \Gamma(x_{1},x_{2},...,x_{n})={\rm det}(X^{*}X)={\rm det}(XX^{*})=\Gamma(y_{1},y_{2},...,y_{m}).

(6.14)

7
Explicit expressions for
C^{-1}(\lambda)
and
(C^{-1}(\lambda)a,a)

In this section we follow
[
30
]
.
Fix
C\in{\rm Mat}(n,\mathbb{R}),

a\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
.
Our aim is to find the explicit formulas for

C^{-1}(\lambda)
and
(C^{-1}(\lambda)a,a)
, where
C(\lambda)
is defined by (
6.6
).
Set
M(i_{1}i_{2}\dots i_{r})(C)

=M^{i_{1}i_{2}\dots i_{r}}_{i_{1}i_{2}\dots i_{r}}(C)
and

a_{i_{1}i_{2}\dots i_{r}}=(a_{i_{1}},a_{i_{2}},\dots,a_{i_{r}})
.
Let also
C_{i_{1}i_{2}\dots i_{r}}
be the corresponding
submatrix
of the matrix
C
.
The elements of this matrix are on the intersection of
i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{r}
rows and column of the matrix
C
.
Denote by
A(C_{i_{1}i_{2}\dots i_{r}})
the matrix of the cofactors of the first order of the matrix
C_{i_{1}i_{2}\dots i_{r}}
, i.e.

A(C_{i_{1}i_{2}\dots i_{r}})=(A^{i}_{j}(C_{i_{1}i_{2}\dots i_{r}}))_{1\leq i,j\leq r}

(7.1)

Let
n=3
, then
A(C_{123})=A(C)
is the following matrix:

A(C)=A(C_{123})=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}A^{1}_{1}&A^{1}_{2}&A^{1}_{3}\\
A^{2}_{1}&A^{2}_{2}&A^{2}_{3}\\
A^{3}_{1}&A^{3}_{2}&A^{3}_{3}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}M^{23}_{23}&-M^{23}_{13}&M^{23}_{12}\\
-M^{13}_{23}&M^{13}_{13}&-M^{13}_{12}\\
M^{12}_{23}&-M^{12}_{13}&M^{12}_{12}\end{array}\right),

(7.2)

where we write
M^{ij}_{rs}
instead of
M^{ij}_{rs}(C)
and
A^{i}_{j}
instead of
A^{i}_{j}(C)
.

Remark 7.1
.

Let
A^{T}
be the transposed matrix
of
A
. Then

A^{T}(C_{i_{1}i_{2}\dots i_{r}})={\rm det}\,C^{-1}_{i_{1}i_{2}\dots i_{r}}\Big(C^{-1}_{i_{1}i_{2}\dots i_{r}}\Big),

(7.3)

In what follows we will consider the submatrix
C_{i_{1}i_{2}\dots i_{r}}
of the matrix
C\in{\rm Mat}(n,\mathbb{R})
as an appropriate element of
{\rm Mat}(n,\mathbb{R})
.

Theorem 7.70
.

For the matrix
C(\lambda)
defined by (
6.6
)

a\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
we have

P_{C}(\lambda)=\Big(\prod_{k=1}^{n}\lambda_{k}\Big)\sum_{r=1}^{n}\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}<\dots<i_{r}\leq n}\frac{M(i_{1}i_{2}\dots i_{r})}{\lambda_{i_{1}}\lambda_{i_{2}}\dots\lambda_{i_{r}}},

(7.4)

C^{-1}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{P_{C}(\lambda)}\Big(\prod_{k=1}^{n}\lambda_{k}\Big)\sum_{r=1}^{n}\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}<\dots i_{r}\leq n}\frac{A(C_{i_{1}i_{2}\dots i_{r}})}{\lambda_{i_{1}}\lambda_{i_{2}}\dots\lambda_{i_{r}}},

(7.5)

(C^{-1}(\lambda)a,a)=\frac{1}{P_{C}(\lambda)}\Big(\prod_{k=1}^{n}\lambda_{k}\Big)\sum_{r=1}^{n}\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}<\dots i_{r}\leq n}\frac{(A(C_{i_{1}i_{2}\dots i_{r}})a_{i_{1}i_{2}\dots i_{r}},a_{i_{1}i_{2}\dots i_{r}})}{\lambda_{i_{1}}\lambda_{i_{2}}\dots\lambda_{i_{r}}}.

(7.6)

7.1
The case where
C
is the Gram matrix

Fix the matrix
X_{mn}
defined by (
6.10
). Denote by
C
the Gram matrix

\gamma(x_{1},x_{2},...,x_{n})
,
i.e.,

C=\gamma(x_{1},x_{2},...,x_{n}),

(7.7)

where
(x_{1},x_{2},...,x_{n})
are defined by (
6.11
)
and
\gamma(x_{1},x_{2},...,x_{n})
by (
6.12
).
In what follows we consider the operator
C(\lambda)
defined
by (
6.6
).

Remark 7.2
.

In this case we have

\displaystyle P_{C}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm det}\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{n}\lambda_{k}E_{kk}+\gamma(x_{1},x_{2},...,x_{n})\Big)

(7.8)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{k=1}^{n}\lambda_{k}\Big(1+\sum_{r=1}^{n}\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}<...<i_{r}\leq m}\Big(\lambda_{i_{1}}\lambda_{i_{2}}...\lambda_{i_{r}}\Big)^{-1}\Gamma(x_{i_{1}},x_{i_{2}},...,x_{i_{r}})\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{k=1}^{n}\lambda_{k}\Big(1+\sum_{r=1}^{n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}<...<i_{r}\leq n;\\
1\leq j_{1}<j_{2}<...<j_{r}\leq n\end{subarray}}\Big(\lambda_{i_{1}}\lambda_{i_{2}}...\lambda_{i_{r}}\Big)^{-1}\Big(M^{i_{1}i_{2}...i_{r}}_{j_{1}j_{2}...j_{r}}(X)\Big)^{2}\Big),

where we have used the following formula (see
[
9
]
, Chap IX, §5 formula (25)):

\Gamma(x_{i_{1}},x_{i_{2}},...,x_{i_{r}})=\sum_{1\leq j_{1}<j_{2}<...<j_{r}\leq m}\left(M^{i_{1}i_{2}...i_{r}}_{j_{1}j_{2}...j_{r}}(X)\right)^{2}.

(7.9)

7.2
Case
m=2

Fix two natural numbers
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
with
m\leq n
, two matrices
A_{mn}
and
X_{mn}
, vectors
g_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{m-1},\,\,1\leq k\leq n

and
a\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
as follows

A_{mn}\!=\!\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}a_{11}&a_{12}&...&a_{1n}\\
a_{21}&a_{22}&...&a_{2n}\\
&&...&\\
a_{m1}&a_{m2}&...&a_{mn}\end{array}\right),\,\,g_{k}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}a_{2k}\\
a_{3k}\\
...\\
a_{mk}\end{array}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{m-1},\,\,\,a=(a_{1k})_{k=1}^{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.

(7.10)

Set
C=\gamma(g_{1},g_{2},\dots,g_{n})
. We calculate
\Delta_{n}(\lambda,C)
and
(C^{-1}(\lambda)a,a)
for an arbitrary
n
.
Consider the matrix (
6.10
)

X_{mn}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}x_{11}&x_{12}&...&x_{1n}\\
x_{21}&x_{22}&...&x_{2n}\\
&&...&\\
x_{m1}&x_{m2}&...&x_{mn}\end{array}\right),\quad\text{where}\quad x_{kr}=\frac{a_{1k}}{\sqrt{\lambda}_{k}},\quad y_{r}=(x_{rk})_{k=1}^{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.

(7.11)

Lemma 7.71

(
[
30
]
, Lemma 2.2)
.

For
m=2
we have

(C^{-1}(\lambda)a,a)=\Delta(y_{1},y_{2})=\frac{\Gamma(y_{1})+\Gamma(y_{1},y_{2})}{1+\Gamma(y_{2})},

(7.12)

where
y_{1}
and
y_{2}
are defined as follows

y_{1}=\left(\frac{a_{1k}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{k}}}\right)_{k=1}^{n}\quad y_{2}=\left(\frac{a_{2k}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{k}}}\right)_{k=1}^{n}.

(7.13)

7.3
Case
m=3

By (
7.8
) we get

\Delta(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})=\frac{\Gamma(y_{1})+\Gamma(y_{1},y_{2})+\Gamma(y_{1},y_{3})+\Gamma(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})}{1+\Gamma(y_{2})+\Gamma(y_{3})+\Gamma(y_{2},y_{3})}.

Lemma 7.72
.

For
m=3
we have

(C^{-1}(\lambda)a,a)=\Delta(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}),

(7.14)

where the
y_{r}
are defined as follows:

y_{r}=\left(\frac{a_{rk}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{k}}}\right)_{k=1}^{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\quad 1\leq r\leq 3.

(7.15)

8
Appendix

8.1
Comparison of two Gaussian measures

For two centered Gaussian measures
\mu_{(b,0)}
and
\mu_{(b^{\prime},0)}
on the real line
\mathbb{R}
defined by (
1.5
) it is well known that

H(\mu_{(b,0)},\mu_{(b^{\prime},0)})=\left(\frac{4bb^{\prime}}{(b+b^{\prime})^{2}}\right)^{1/4}.

(8.1)

By Kakutani’s criterion for product measures on
\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}

[
13
]
, and (
8.1
) we see that the following lemma holds true.

Lemma 8.73
.

Two Gaussian measures
\mu_{(b,0)}=\otimes_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mu_{(b,0)}
and
\mu_{(b^{\prime},0)}=\otimes_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mu_{(b^{\prime},0)}
are equivalent if and only if the product

\prod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{4b_{n}b^{\prime}_{n}}{(b_{n}+b^{\prime}_{n})^{2}}

(8.2)

does not converge to
0
. The equivalent condition is

\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{b_{n}}{b^{\prime}_{n}}}-\sqrt{\frac{b^{\prime}_{n}}{b_{n}}}\right)^{2}<\infty.

(8.3)

Consider
two measures:
\mu_{({\mathbb{I}},0)}=\otimes_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mu_{(1,0)}

and
\mu_{({\mathbb{I}}+c,0)}=\otimes_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mu_{(1+c_{n},0)}
on the space
X_{1}
, where the measure
\mu_{(b,a)}
on the real line
\mathbb{R}
is defined by (
1.5
).

Lemma 8.74
.

Two measures
\mu_{({\mathbb{I}},0)}
and
\mu_{({\mathbb{I}}+c,0)}
are equivalent if and only if

\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}c_{n}^{2}<\infty

(8.4)

Proof 37
.

By Lemma
8.73
and
(
8.3
), the measures
\mu_{({\mathbb{I}},0)}
and
\mu_{({\mathbb{I}}+c,0)}

are equivalent if and only if

\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+c_{n}}}-\sqrt{1+c_{n}}\right)^{2}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{c_{n}^{2}}{1+c_{n}}<\infty.

By Lemma
2.8
,
two series
\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{c_{n}^{2}}{1+c_{n}}
and
\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}c_{n}^{2}
are equivalent.
∎

The next lemma is also a consequence of Kakutani’s criterion
[
13
]
.

Lemma 8.75
.

Two Gaussian measures
\mu_{(b,0)}^{m}
and
\mu_{(b^{\prime},0)}^{m}
are equivalent if and only if the product

\prod_{r=1}^{m}\prod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{4b_{rn}b^{\prime}_{rn}}{(b_{rn}+b^{\prime}_{rn})^{2}}

(8.5)

does not converge to
0
. The equivalent condition is

\sum_{r=1}^{m}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{b_{rn}}{b^{\prime}_{rn}}}-\sqrt{\frac{b^{\prime}_{rn}}{b_{rn}}}\right)^{2}<\infty.

(8.6)

Lemma
1.2
follows from Lemmas
8.76
–
8.79
.

Lemma 8.76
.

For
t\!\in\!{\rm GL}(m,{\mathbb{R}})\backslash\{e\}
we have

(\mu_{(b,a)}^{m})^{L_{t}}\!\perp\!\mu_{(b,a)}^{m}
if and only if

(\mu_{(b,0)}^{m})^{L_{t}}\perp\mu_{(b,0)}^{m}\quad\text{or}\quad\mu_{(b,{L_{t}}a)}^{m}\perp\mu_{(b,a)}^{m}.

(8.7)

Let us define the following measures on the spaces
{\mathbb{R}}^{m}

and
X_{m}
:

\mu_{m}^{(B_{n},0)}=\otimes_{k=1}^{m}\mu_{(b_{kn},0)},\quad\mu_{m}^{(B_{n},a_{n})}=\otimes_{k=1}^{m}\mu_{(b_{kn},a_{kn})},

where
a_{n}\!=\!(a_{1n},...,a_{mn})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
and

B_{n}\!=\!{\rm diag}(b_{1n},...,b_{mn})\in{\rm Mat}(m,{\mathbb{R}})
. Since

\mu_{(b,a)}^{m}=\otimes_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\mu_{m}^{(B_{n},a_{n})},\quad\mu_{(b,0)}^{m}=\otimes_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\mu_{m}^{(B_{n},0)},

\left(\mu_{(b,a)}^{m}\right)^{L_{t}}=\otimes_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\left(\mu_{m}^{(B_{n},a_{n})}\right)^{L_{t}},\quad\left(\mu_{(b,0)}^{m}\right)^{L_{t}}=\otimes_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\left(\mu_{m}^{(B_{n},0)}\right)^{L_{t}},

and

\mu_{(b,{L_{t}}a)}^{m}=\otimes_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\mu_{m}^{(B_{n},{L_{t}}a_{n})},

by the Kakutani criterion
[
13
]
, we derive the following two lemmas:

Lemma 8.77
.

For the measures
\mu_{(b,0)}^{m},\,m\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
t\in{\rm GL}(m,{\mathbb{R}})\backslash\{e\}
, we obtain

(\mu_{(b,0)}^{m})^{L_{t}}\perp\mu_{(b,0)}^{m}\,\,\Leftrightarrow\prod_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}H\left(\left(\mu_{m}^{(B_{n},0)}\right)^{L_{t}},\mu_{m}^{(B_{n},0)}\right)=0.

Lemma 8.78
.

For the measures
\mu_{(b,0)}^{m},\,m\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
t\in{\rm GL}(m,{\mathbb{R}})\backslash\{e\}
, we get

\mu_{(b,{L_{t}}a)}^{m}\perp\mu_{(b,a)}^{m}\Leftrightarrow\prod_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}H\left(\mu_{m}^{(B_{n},{L_{t}}a_{n})},\mu_{m}^{(B_{n},a_{n})}\right)=0.

To prove Lemma
1.2
it is sufficient to show, by Lemma

8.76
, that

H_{m,n}(t)\!=\!H\Big(\Big(\mu_{m}^{(B_{n},0)}\Big)^{L_{t}}\!\!\!\!,\mu_{m}^{(B_{n},0)}\Big)\!=\!\Big(\frac{1}{2^{m}|{\rm det}\,\,t|}{\rm det}\left(I\!+\!X_{n}^{*}(t)X_{n}(t)\right)\Big)^{-1/2}\!\!\!\!\!,\quad

(8.8)

to prove the equivalence

\prod_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}H\left(\mu_{m}^{(B_{n},{L_{t}}a_{n})},\mu_{m}^{(B_{n},a_{n})}\right)=0\Leftrightarrow\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\sum_{r=1}^{m}b_{rn}\Big(\sum_{s=1}^{m}(t_{rs}-\delta_{rs})a_{sn}\Big)^{2}=\infty,

(8.9)

and to apply the following lemma.

Lemma 8.79
.

For
X\in{\rm Mat}(m,{\mathbb{R}})
we have

{\rm det}\left(I+X^{*}X\right)=1+\sum_{r=1}^{m}\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}<...<i_{r}\leq m;1\leq j_{1}<j_{2}<...<j_{r}\leq m}\left(M^{i_{1}i_{2}...i_{r}}_{j_{1}j_{2}...j_{r}}(X)\right)^{2}.

(8.10)

The proof of the equivalence (
8.9
) is based on the following theorem
that one can find, e.g., in
[
35
, Ch. III, §16, Theorem 2]
.

Theorem 8.80
.

Two Gaussian measures
\mu_{B,a}
and
\mu_{B,b}
in a Hilbert space
H
are
equivalent if and only of
B^{-1/2}(a-b)\in H
.

Indeed, we have

\|C^{-1/2}(ta-a)\|^{2}_{H}\!=\!\!\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\|C_{n}^{-1/2}(t-I)a_{n}\|^{2}_{H_{n}}\!=\!2\!\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\sum_{r=1}^{m}\frac{b_{kn}}{d_{kn}}\Big(\sum_{s=1}^{m}(t_{rs}\!-\delta_{rs})a_{sn}\Big)^{2}\!d_{kn}.

To explain the latter equality let us describe
H
and
C
. To
find an operator
C
we present the measure
\mu^{m}_{(b,a)}
in the
canonical form
\mu_{C,a}
defined by its Fourier transform:

\int_{H}\exp i(y,x)d\mu_{C,a}(x)=\exp\left(i(a,y)-\frac{1}{2}(Cy,y)\right),\,\,y\in H,

(8.11)

where
C
is a positive
nuclear operator
(called the
covariance operator
) on the Hilbert space
H
, and
a\in H
is
the
mathematical expectation
or
mean
.

Recall the
Kolmogorov zero-one law
. Let us consider in the space
{\mathbb{R}}^{\infty}={\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}\times\cdots
the infinite tensor product
\mu_{b}=\otimes_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\mu_{b_{k}}
of one-dimensional Gaussian measures
\mu_{b_{k}}
on
\mathbb{R}
defined as follows:

d\mu_{b}(x)=\sqrt{\frac{b}{\pi}}\exp(-bx^{2})dx.

(8.12)

Consider a Hilbert space
l_{2}(a)
defined by

l_{2}(a)=\big\{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\infty}\,:\,\|x\|^{2}_{l_{2}(a)}=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}x_{k}^{2}a_{k}<\infty\big\},

where
a=(a_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is an infinite sequence of
positive numbers.

Theorem 8.81

(Kolmogorov’s zero-one law,
[
34
]
)
.

We have

\mu_{b}(l_{2}(a))=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}0,&\text{if}&\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}\frac{a_{k}}{b_{k}}=\infty;\\
1,&&\text{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

8.2
Properties of two vectors
f,g\not\in l_{2}

In what follows we will use systematically the following notation.
For
k
vectors
f_{1},f_{2},\dots,f_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
k\leq n
we set

\Delta(f_{1},f_{2},\dots,f_{k})=\frac{{\rm det}(I+\gamma(f_{1},f_{2},\dots,f_{k}))}{{\rm det}(I+\gamma(f_{2},\dots,f_{k}))}-1.

(8.13)

For
k=2
and
k=3
we get respectively:

\Delta(f_{1},f_{2})=\frac{{\rm det}(I+\gamma(f_{1},f_{2}))}{{\rm det}(I+\gamma(f_{2}))}-1=\frac{I+\Gamma(f_{1})+\Gamma(f_{2})+\Gamma(f_{1},f_{2})}{I+\Gamma(f_{2})}.

(8.14)

\displaystyle\Delta(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})=\frac{{\rm det}(I+\gamma(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}))}{{\rm det}(I+\gamma(f_{2},f_{3}))}-1=

(8.15)

\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma(f_{1})+\Gamma(f_{1},f_{2})+\Gamma(f_{1},f_{3})+\Gamma(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})}{1+\Gamma(f_{2})+\Gamma(f_{3})+\Gamma(f_{2},f_{3})}.

Lemma 8.82

(
[
28
]
, and
[
27
]
, Ch.10)
.

Let
f=(f_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
and
g=(g_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be two real vectors such that

\|f\|^{2}=\infty
, where
\|f\|^{2}=\sum_{k}f_{k}^{2}
. Denote by
f_{(n)}
,

g_{(n)}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
their projections to the subspace

{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, i.e.,
f_{(n)}=(f_{k})_{k=1}^{n},\quad g_{(n)}=(g_{k})_{k=1}^{n}
. Then

\Delta(f,g):=\lim_{n\to\infty}\Delta(f_{(n)},g_{(n)})=\infty,

(8.16)

where
\Delta(f_{(n)},g_{(n)})
is defined by (
8.14
),
in the following cases:

\displaystyle(a)

\displaystyle\|g\|^{2}<\infty,

\displaystyle(b)

\displaystyle\|g\|^{2}=\infty,\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\|f_{(n)}\|}{\|g_{(n)}\|}=\infty,

\displaystyle(c)

\displaystyle\|f\|^{2}=\|g\|^{2}=\|f+sg\|^{2}=\infty,\quad\text{for all}\quad s\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}.

Proof 38
.

Obviously
\lim_{n\to\infty}\Delta(f_{(n)},g_{(n)})=\infty
if conditions (a) or (b) hold.
The implication
(c)\Rightarrow(\ref{Delta-to-infty})
is based on the following lemma.
∎

Lemma 8.83
.

Let
f=(f_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
and
g=(g_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be two real vectors such that

\|f\|^{2}=\|g\|^{2}=\|C_{1}f+C_{2}g\|^{2}=\infty\quad\text{for all}\quad(C_{1},C_{2})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\setminus\{0\},

(8.17)

\text{then}\quad\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\Gamma(f_{(n)},g_{(n)})}{\Gamma(g_{(n)})}=\infty\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\Gamma(f_{(n)},g_{(n)})}{\Gamma(f_{(n)})}=\infty.

(8.18)

Lemma 8.84
.

Let
f_{1},f_{2}\not\in l_{2}
and

\Delta(f_{1},f_{2})<\infty
, then for some

(C_{1},C_{2})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\setminus\{0\}
we have

C_{1}f_{1}+C_{2}f_{2}\in l_{2}
.

Proof 39
.

Let assume the opposite, i.e.,
C_{1}f_{1}+C_{2}f_{2}\not\in l_{2}
for all
(C_{1},C_{2})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\setminus\{0\}
.
Then by Lemma
8.83

\hskip 36.98866pt\Delta(f_{1},f_{2})=\frac{\Gamma(f_{1})+\Gamma(f_{1},f_{2})}{1+\Gamma(f_{2})}>\frac{\Gamma(f_{1},f_{2})}{1+\Gamma(f_{2})}\sim\frac{\Gamma(f_{1},f_{2})}{\Gamma(f_{2})}=\infty.\hskip 36.98866pt\Box

Lemma 8.85
.

Let
f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\not\in l_{2}
and

\Delta(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})<\infty
, then for some

(C_{1},C_{2},C_{3})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\setminus\{0\}
we have

C_{1}f_{1}+C_{2}f_{2}+C_{3}f_{3}\in l_{2}
.

Proof 40
.

Let assume the opposite, i.e., that
C_{1}f_{1}+C_{2}f_{2}+C_{3}f_{3}\not\in l_{2}
for all
(C_{1},C_{2},C_{3})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\setminus\{0\}
.
Then by Lemmas
8.87
–
8.83

and (
8.15
) we get

\displaystyle\Delta(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})=\frac{\Gamma(f_{1})+\Gamma(f_{1},f_{2})+\Gamma(f_{1},f_{3})+\Gamma(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})}{1+\Gamma(f_{2})+\Gamma(f_{3})+\Gamma(f_{2},f_{3})}>

\displaystyle\hskip 54.06006pt\frac{\Gamma(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})}{1+\Gamma(f_{2})+\Gamma(f_{3})+\Gamma(f_{2},f_{3})}\sim\frac{\Gamma(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})}{\Gamma(f_{2},f_{3})}=\infty.\hskip 54.06006pt\Box

8.3
Properties of three vectors
f,g,h\not\in l_{2}

Lemma 8.86
.

Let
f=(f_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}},\,\,g=(g_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
and
h=(h_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be three real vectors such that

\|f\|^{2}=\infty
where
\|f\|^{2}=\sum_{k}f_{k}^{2}
. Denote by
f_{(n)},\,\,g_{(n)},\,\,h_{(n)}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}

their projections to the subspace
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, i.e.,
f_{(n)}=(f_{k})_{k=1}^{n},\,g_{(n)}=(g_{k})_{k=1}^{n},\,\,

h_{(n)}=(h_{k})_{k=1}^{n}
. Then

\Delta(f,g,h):=\lim_{n\to\infty}\Delta(f_{(n)},g_{(n)},h_{(n)})=\infty,

(8.19)

where
\Delta(f,g,h)
is defined by (
8.15
), in the following cases:

\displaystyle(a)

\displaystyle\|g\|^{2}<\infty\quad\text{and}\quad\|h\|^{2}<\infty,

\displaystyle(b)

\displaystyle\|g\|^{2}<\infty\quad\text{and}\quad\|h\|^{2}=\infty,\,\,\text{or}\,\,\|g\|^{2}=\infty\quad\text{and}\quad\|h\|^{2}<\infty,

\displaystyle(c)

\displaystyle\|C_{1}f+C_{2}g+C_{3}h\|^{2}=\infty,\,\,\,\text{for all}\,\,\,(C_{1},C_{2},C_{3})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\setminus\{0\}.

Proof 41
.

(a) In this case
\|g_{(n)}\|^{2}\leq C,\,\,\,\|h_{(n)}\|^{2}\leq C
and
\Gamma(g_{(n)},h_{(n)})\leq C

and therefore,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\Delta(f_{(n)},g_{(n)},h_{(n)})\geq\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\|f_{(n)}\|^{2}}{1+3C}=\infty.

(b) Let
\|g\|^{2}<\infty,\,\,\|h\|^{2}=\infty
. In this case we have

\Gamma(g_{(n)},h_{(n)})\leq\|g_{(n)}\|^{2}\|h_{(n)}\|^{2}\sin^{2}(\alpha_{n})\leq C_{1}\Gamma(h_{(n)}),

where
\alpha_{n}
is the angle betwen two vectors
g_{(n)}
and
h_{(n)}
.
Therefore,

1+\Gamma(g_{(n)})+\Gamma(h_{(n)})+\Gamma(g_{(n)},h_{(n)})\leq(1+C)(1+\Gamma(h_{(n)}),

\Delta(f_{(n)},g_{(n)},h_{(n)})\geq\frac{\Gamma(f_{(n)})+\Gamma(f_{(n)},h_{(n)})}{(1+C)(1+\Gamma(h_{(n)})}\sim\Delta(f_{(n)},h_{(n)}).

So, this case is reduced to the case
m=2
, see Lemma
8.83
.

The implication
(c)\Rightarrow(\ref{Delta-to-infty.3})
is based on the following lemma
(the proof of which will appear in
[
29
]
. Compare with Lemma
8.82
.
∎

Lemma 8.87

(see
[
29
]
)
.

Let
f_{0},f_{1},f_{2}
be three infinite real vectors

f_{r}=(f_{rk})_{k\in\mathbb{N}},

0\leq r\leq 2

such that for all
(C_{0},C_{1},C_{2})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\setminus\{0\}
holds

\sum_{r=0}^{2}C_{r}f_{r}\not\in l_{2},\quad\text{i.e.,}\quad\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}|C_{0}f_{0k}+C_{1}f_{1k}+C_{2}f_{2k}|^{2}=\infty.

(8.20)

Denote by
f_{r}(n)=(f_{rk})_{k=1}^{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
the projections of the vectors
f_{r}
on the subspace
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then

\frac{\Gamma(f_{0},f_{1},f_{2})}{\Gamma(f_{1},f_{2})}:=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\Gamma(f_{0}(n),f_{1}(n),f_{2}(n))}{\Gamma(f_{1}(n),f_{2}(n))}=\infty.

(8.21)

The idea of the proof
(for details see
[
29
]
).
We assume there exists a real number
C
such that

\quad\quad\frac{\Gamma(f_{0}(n),f_{1}(n),f_{2}(n))}{\Gamma(f_{1}(n),f_{2}(n))}\leq C,

(8.22)

for every integer
n
and will show that this leads to a contradiction.

For
t\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
f_{0},f_{1},f_{2}\in H
we define the function

\displaystyle F_{20}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|\sum_{k=1}^{2}t_{k}f_{k}-f_{0}\|^{2}=\sum_{k,r=1}^{2}t_{k}t_{r}(f_{k},f_{r})-2\sum_{k=1}^{2}t_{k}(f_{k},f_{0})+(f_{0},f_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(At,t)-2(t,b)+(f_{0},f_{0}),

where
b=(f_{k},f_{0})_{k=1}^{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
A
is the Gram matrix

A=\gamma(f_{1},f_{2})=\big((f_{k},f_{r})\big)_{k,r=1}^{2}.

Suppose that
At_{0}=b
, then we have

(At,t)-2(t,b)+(f_{0},f_{0})=(A(t-t_{0}),(t-t_{0}))+\frac{\Gamma(f_{0},f_{1},f_{2})}{\Gamma(f_{1},f_{2})},

and therefore

\displaystyle F_{20}(t)=\big(A(t-t_{0}),(t-t_{0})\big)+\frac{\Gamma(f_{0},f_{1},f_{2})}{\Gamma(f_{1},f_{2})},

\displaystyle F_{20}(t_{0})=\min_{(t_{1},t_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\|\sum_{k=1}^{2}t_{k}f_{k}-f_{0}\|^{2}=\frac{\Gamma(f_{0},f_{1},f_{2})}{\Gamma(f_{1},f_{2})},

(8.23)

Consider the matrix

X_{3n}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}f_{11}&f_{12}&\dots&f_{1n}\\
f_{21}&f_{22}&\dots&f_{2n}\\
f_{01}&f_{02}&\dots&f_{0n}\\
\end{array}\right)

(8.24)

and its minors

M_{krs}^{123}=\left|\begin{array}[]{ccc}f_{1k}&f_{1r}&f_{1s}\\
f_{2k}&f_{2r}&f_{2s}\\
f_{0k}&f_{0r}&f_{0s}\\
\end{array}\right|,\quad M_{kr}^{12}=\left|\begin{array}[]{cc}f_{1k}&f_{1r}\\
f_{2k}&f_{2r}\end{array}\right|.

Then by

[
9
]

we have

\displaystyle\Gamma(f_{1}(n),f_{2}(n))=\sum_{1\leq r<s\leq n}|M_{rs}^{12}|^{2},

\displaystyle\Gamma(f_{0}(n),f_{1}(n),f_{2}(n))=\sum_{1\leq k<r<s\leq n}|M_{krs}^{123}|^{2}.

Therefore, the inequality (
8.22
)
will have the following form

\frac{\Gamma(f_{0}(n),f_{1}(n),f_{2}(n))}{\Gamma(f_{1}(n),f_{2}(n))}=\frac{\sum_{1\leq k<r<s\leq n}|M_{krs}^{123}|^{2}}{\sum_{1\leq r<s\leq n}|M_{rs}^{12}|^{2}}\leq C

(8.25)

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Set now

\displaystyle a_{2n}=\gamma(f_{1}(n),f_{2}(n),f_{0}(n)),

\displaystyle A_{2n}=\gamma(f_{1}(n),f_{2}(n)),\quad A_{2n}t_{0}^{(n)}=b_{2n},

(8.26)

\displaystyle b_{2n}=(f_{k}(n),f_{0}(n))_{k=1}^{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\quad t_{0}^{(n)}=(t_{0r}^{(n)})_{r=1}^{2}.

(8.27)

More explicitly

a_{2n}\!=\!\left(\!\!\begin{array}[]{ccc}(f_{1}(n),f_{1}(n))&(f_{1}(n),f_{2}(n))&(f_{1}(n),f_{0}(n))\\
(f_{2}(n),f_{1}(n))&(f_{2}(n),f_{2}(n))&(f_{2}(n),f_{0}(n))\\
(f_{0}(n),f_{1}(n))&(f_{0}(n),f_{2}(n))&(f_{0}(n),f_{0}(n))\end{array}\!\!\right),

(8.28)

A_{2n}\!=\!\left(\!\!\begin{array}[]{cc}(f_{1}(n),f_{1}(n))&(f_{1}(n),f_{2}(n))\\
(f_{2}(n),f_{1}(n))&(f_{2}(n),f_{2}(n))\\
\end{array}\!\!\right).

(8.29)

If we replace the vectors
f_{0},f_{1},f_{2}
with
f_{0}(n),f_{1}(n),f_{2}(n)
,
the equality (
8.23
) then becomes

F^{(n)}_{20}(t^{(n)}_{0})=\min_{(t_{1},t_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\|\sum_{k=1}^{2}t_{k}f_{k}(n)-f_{0}(n)\|^{2}=\frac{\Gamma(f_{0}(n),f_{1}(n),f_{2}(n))}{\Gamma(f_{1}(n),f_{2}(n))}.

(8.30)

For
t\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
f_{0}(n),f_{1}(n),f_{2}(n)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

and
0\leq s\leq 2
, define the functions

F^{(n)}_{2s}(t)=\|\sum_{0\leq r\leq 2,r\not=s}t_{r}f_{r}(n)-f_{s}(n)\|^{2}.

(8.31)

The minimum of the corresponding
expressions
F^{(n)}_{2s}(t)
for
0\leq s\leq 2
is attained respectively at
t^{(n)}_{0},\,t^{(n)}_{1},\,t^{(n)}_{2}
.
The proof of the fact that one of the sequences

t^{(n)}_{0},\,t^{(n)}_{1},\,t^{(n)}_{2}

is bounded, is based on the positive definiteness
of the matrices

\gamma(f_{1}(n),f_{2}(n),f_{0}(n))
, for the details see
[
29
]
. Let for example, the sequence
t^{(n)}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
is bounded. Therefore, there exists a subsequence
(t^{(n_{k})})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
that converges to some
t\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. This contradics (
8.22
). Indeed

\lim_{n\to\infty}F^{(n)}_{20}(t)=\infty,\quad F^{(n)}_{20}(t_{0}^{(n_{k})})\leq C,\quad\lim_{k\to\infty}t_{0}^{(n_{k})}=t.

(8.32)
